A handbook of holographic 4-point functions
Adam Bzowski, Paul McFadden, and Kostas Skenderis

Abstract
We present a comprehensive discussion of tree-level holographic 4 4 -point functions of scalar operators in momentum space. We show that each individual Witten diagram satisfies the conformal Ward identities on its own and is thus a valid conformal correlator. When the β = Δ − d / 2 \beta=\Delta-d/2 are half-integral, with Δ \Delta the dimensions of the operators and d d the spacetime dimension, the Witten diagrams can be evaluated in closed form and we present explicit formulae for the case d = 3 d=3 and Δ = 2 , 3 \Delta=2,3 . These correlators require renormalization, which we carry out explicitly, and lead to new conformal anomalies and beta functions. Correlators of operators of different dimension may be linked via weight-shifting operators, which allow new correlators to be generated from given ‘seed’ correlators. We present a new derivation of weight-shifting operators in momentum space and uncover several subtleties associated with their use: such operators map exchange diagrams to a linear combination of exchange and contact diagrams, and special care must be taken when renormalization is required.



Introduction

Witten diagrams have become an indispensable tool in analyzing CFTs at strong coupling. Yet explicit closed formulae in momentum space are rare.
This is even more the case when the correlators require renormalization. It is the purpose of this paper to show how to obtain such formulae for tree-level holographic
4
-point functions of operators of dimension
\Delta
, with
\Delta
such that the combinations
\beta=\Delta-d/2
are half-integral, where
d
is the spacetime dimension.

The simplest cases, which are still of considerable interest, are those of 4-point functions of scalar operators of dimension
\Delta=2,3
in three-dimensional CFTs. For these cases, we discuss and present explicit formulae for all holographic CFT
4
-point functions in momentum space that can be constructed from tree-level contact and exchange Witten diagrams, with exchanged scalar operators of dimension
\Delta=2,3
. The methodology we discuss extends to other operators and spacetime dimensions (provided the
\beta
are half-integral) and the Mathematica file we supply may be adapted to such cases.

Our focus on
4
-point functions of scalar operators with dimensions
\Delta=2,3
in three spacetime dimensions is motivated in part by recent works on
the cosmological bootstrap
Arkani-Hamed:2015bza
;
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Baumann:2019oyu
;
Baumann:2020dch
;
Baumann:2021fxj
;
Sleight:2019mgd
;
Sleight:2019hfp
;
Sleight:2020obc
;
Sleight:2021plv
;
DiPietro:2021sjt
;
Meltzer:2021zin
, in which late-time correlators in four-dimensional de Sitter spacetime are constructed by solving three-dimensional conformal Ward identities. These operators are dual to conformally coupled and massless bulk scalars, with the latter modelling the inflaton.
The same approach was also explored at the level of the
3
-point function in the earlier holographic cosmology literature
Maldacena:2011nz
;
Bzowski:2012ih
;
Mata:2012bx
;
McFadden:2013ria
;
Anninos:2014lwa
. The correlation functions of these operators require renormalization and
a key question is whether the divergences affecting the corresponding
4
-point correlators in anti-de Sitter spacetime, as studied here, also play a role in de Sitter. This question will be addressed in a follow-up paper
toappear
using the results for renormalized AdS/CFT correlators developed here.

Witten diagrams
Witten:1998qj
were historically computed in position space, see
Freedman:1998tz
;
Liu:1998ty
;
DHoker:1999kzh
;
Arutyunov:2000py
;
Dolan:2004iy
for a sample of computations from the early days of AdS/CFT.
However, the correlators we discuss here contain divergences as tabulated in Table
1
, and one of the central aims of this work is to provide a complete, yet readable, account of their renormalization. The extraction of divergences is best performed in momentum space
Bzowski:2015pba
;
Bzowski:2017poo
;
Bzowski:2018fql
, and as such we work throughout in momentum space. The momentum-space results are also of current interest for many applications
including inflationary cosmology.
As the Fourier transform is well-defined after renormalization, our results can in principle also be transformed back to position space.

The framework we use for CFT in momentum space was introduced in
Bzowski:2013sza
. The general structure of 4-point functions in momentum space was given in
Bzowski:2019kwd
;
Bzowski:2020kfw
and a number of special cases were discussed
in
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Isono:2018rrb
;
Albayrak:2018tam
;
Albayrak:2019asr
;
Maglio:2019grh
;
Isono:2019wex
;
Coriano:2019nkw
;
Albayrak:2020isk
.
1
1

Anand:2019lkt
;

;
Jain:2021wyn

;

.

Our hope is that this handbook of renormalized holographic
4
-point functions, which we believe is the most comprehensive
of its kind, will be of use to a wide range of researchers. To this end,
we provide full documentation including a set of accompanying Mathematica notebooks.
Researchers interested in the
renormalization of CFT correlators
will find a complete discussion of tree-level
4
-point functions, while those seeking specific results need only consult the relevant listings or notebooks.

Table 1:
Degree of divergence
n
for Witten diagrams dimensionally regulated according to the scheme (
1
). Each diagram diverges as
\epsilon^{-n}
for
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, and is labelled by the dimensions
[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]
of the external operators
and that of the exchanged operator
\Delta_{x}
where present.

Aside from their applications, an additional reason for focusing on correlators with
\Delta=2,3
in
d=3
is the relative simplicity of their construction: the corresponding Witten diagrams feature propagators involving Bessel functions with half-integer indices. The latter reduce to elementary functions enabling all diagrams to be easily evaluated in terms of dilogarithms. It is desirable to preserve this simplicity in the regulated theory, and we show this is possible by regulating the spacetime and operator dimensions as

\displaystyle d\longmapsto\hat{d}=d+2\epsilon,\qquad\Delta_{j}\longmapsto\hat{\Delta}_{j}=\Delta_{j}+\epsilon,

(1)

where
j
runs over all external and exchanged (‘
x
’) operators
j=1,2,3,4,x
and
\epsilon
is the regulator.
This ‘half-integer’ scheme preserves the indices on all Bessel functions, which are given by
\Delta_{j}-d/2=\hat{\Delta}_{j}-\hat{d}/2
, and is sufficient to regulate all the divergences we encounter here.
Renormalization can then be achieved by constructing suitable counterterms directly within the boundary CFT.

For completeness, we also explain how to change to a fully general dimensional regularization scheme

\displaystyle d\longmapsto\hat{d}=d+2u\epsilon,\qquad\Delta_{j}\longmapsto\hat{\Delta}_{j}=\Delta_{j}+(u+v_{j})\epsilon,

(2)

where
u
and
v_{j}
, for
j=1,2,3,4,x
, are real constants parametrizing the scheme. This can be achieved by expanding the Bessel functions in their indices, for which several results are available.
An alternative to both the above approaches would be to employ holographic renormalization
Skenderis:2002wp
, however in practice the resulting cut-off radial integrals are less convenient to evaluate than those encountered in dimensional regularization.

Usually in quantum field theory individual Feynman diagrams are not physically meaningful and do not satisfy Ward identities on their own. Instead, one must sum all relevant diagrams to construct observable quantities, such as correlators, that satisfy the relevant Ward identities.
However, an interesting general feature of tree-level Witten diagrams associated with correlators of scalar operators is that they
individually
satisfy the conformal Ward identities.
2
2

2

We emphasize that this property does
not
hold for Witten diagrams associated with correlators of non-abelian symmetry currents and/or the energy-momentum tensor. In such cases the corresponding bulk action involves a (bulk) gauge symmetry and summing over all Witten diagrams is necessary in order to get a physically meaningful answer for the correlator.

This follows from that the fact that there is a choice of bulk AdS Lagrangian such that any given Witten diagram is the only diagram contributing to a corresponding correlator. We illustrate this in the cases we analyze, and we expect this to be a general feature. The existence of a bulk Lagrangian implies that the diagram must satisfy the conformal Ward identities, as these follow from the bulk isometries, and also that we should be able to renormalize at the level of individual Witten diagrams.
This does not necessarily mean however that there exists an actual CFT with the chosen spectrum
and interaction. Where such a CFT does not exist (for example, via bootstrap arguments) then the corresponding bulk AdS Lagrangian would be in the swampland.

An interesting approach to the construction of correlators is to use weight-shifting operators. These operators were introduced in position space in
Karateev:2017jgd
(see also
Costa:2018mcg
), and by construction, map CFT correlators of operators of given conformal dimensions to those of operators with different (shifted) conformal dimensions. One may thus start from ‘seed’ correlators and obtain others using weight-shifting operators. These were applied in momentum space in
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Baumann:2019oyu
and we further develop and analyze this approach here.
There are however a number of subtleties that need to be taken into account when using this method. First, the application of weight-shifting operators to exchange diagrams generally produces a linear combination of shifted exchange and contact diagrams, rather than a pure exchange diagram. So if one wants to obtain exchange diagrams using weight-shifting operators one needs to know first all the contact diagrams. Second, care is needed when renormalization is required: renormalized correlators obey inhomogeneous conformal Ward identities containing anomalies and beta functions, whereas these operators only connect solutions of the homogeneous conformal Ward identities. In particular, one cannot construct a renormalized correlator, which depends on the RG scale
\mu
, by acting with a (
\mu
-independent) weight-shifting operator on a finite correlator, since the latter has no
\mu
-dependence.
One may apply weight-shifting operators at the level of the regulated theory, but only a subset of them yield useful relations as sometimes weight-shifting operators map regulated exchange diagrams to shifted contact diagrams only.

For the cases we analyze, one may use weight-shifting operators to reduce all exchange diagrams to a single diagram per exchanged scalar (
\Delta_{x}=2
and
\Delta_{x}=3
), plus all contact diagrams. One may further link the two master exchange integrals via a different relation, so that at the end all exchange integrals may be reduced to the exchange diagram with all external dimensions, and the dimension of the exchanged scalar, equal to three (plus all contact diagrams).
However, while all obstacles have been avoided and the regulated amplitudes constructed, the resulting scheme is little simpler than evaluating the diagrams directly, at least for the cases we analyze.

The layout of this paper is as follows. In the next section we present the objects of interest: the regulated Witten diagrams. Section
3
proceeds to evaluate these diagrams in the ‘half-integer’ scheme (
1
), after which they are renormalized as described in Section
4
. Section
5
discusses how to change to the general regularization scheme (
2
), and provides a complete list of such results. The discussion of the change of scheme is the most complicated part of this paper. Readers interested only in results may skip this part.
The action of weight-shifting operators is discussed in Section
6
. We present a simple momentum-space derivation of these operators based on the shadow transform, then give a new formula for their action on exchange diagrams before discussing their limitations when applied to renormalized correlators.
Section
7
summarises the Mathematica packages accompanying this paper, which contain a complete record of all our results. We discuss the outlook in Section
8
. The paper contains four appendices. In Appendix
A
we summarize our conventions and definitions for momenta. Appendix
B
lists the conformal Ward identities and
Appendix
C
various useful mathematical results. Finally, in Appendix
D
we present additional results on weight-shifting operators.

Definitions for regulated amplitudes

Our goal is to calculate the
amplitudes
,
i.e.
, the
3
- and
4
-point
Witten diagrams
, as presented in Figures
1
and
2
. As usual, external and internal lines correspond to bulk-to-boundary and bulk-to-bulk propagators in pure Euclidean AdS, while each interior point requires integration over its radial position. All our conventions for QFT and the momenta are summarized in Appendix
A
.
At this point, we will not make reference to any specific bulk action: rather, we will simply focus on the individual amplitudes defined by the expressions below, postponing consideration of their relation to the bulk action.

\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{k}_{1})
\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{k}_{3})
\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{k}_{2})

The scalar bulk-to-boundary propagator is

\displaystyle\K_{d,\Delta}(z,k)=\frac{k^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}z^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(kz)}{2^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}-1}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\right)}

(3)

while the scalar bulk-to-bulk propagator is

\displaystyle\G_{d,\Delta}(z,k;\zeta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(z\zeta)^{\frac{d}{2}}I_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(kz)K_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(k\zeta)&\text{ for }z<\zeta,\\
(z\zeta)^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(kz)I_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(k\zeta)&\text{ for }z>\zeta.\end{array}\right.

where
I_{\beta}
and
K_{\beta}
are the modified Bessel functions.

To avoid clutter, we will use
\hat{\K}_{[\Delta]}
and
\hat{\G}_{[\Delta]}
to denote the propagators for the regulated parameters
\hat{d}
and
\hat{\Delta}
defined in (
2
), leaving the specific scheme implicit.

•

For convenience, regulated 2-point amplitudes are normalized so as to match the holographic 2-point functions:

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[\Delta\Delta]}(k)=(2\hat{\Delta}-\hat{d})\times\text{coefficient of }z^{\hat{\Delta}}\text{ in }\hat{\K}_{[\Delta]}(z,k).

(6)

All non-diagonal 2-point amplitudes
\hat{i}_{[\Delta\Delta^{\prime}]}
with
\Delta\neq\Delta^{\prime}
vanish.

•

We define the regulated 3-point amplitudes as

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}z\,z^{-\hat{d}-1}\,\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(z,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(z,k_{2})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(z,k_{3}).

(7)

.

Many of the regularized amplitudes defined above exhibit divergences as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. The 3-point amplitudes
\hat{i}_{[222]},\hat{i}_{[322]},\hat{i}_{[332]},\hat{i}_{[333]}
all have a single pole
\epsilon^{-1}
. For the 4-point amplitudes, the degrees of divergence are summarized in Table
1
.

Regulated amplitudes in the half-integer scheme

The idea of the half-integer scheme (
1
) is that all the Bessel functions appearing in the propagators for operators of dimensions
\Delta=2
and
\Delta=3
reduce to elementary functions. Explicitly, the bulk-to-boundary propagators (
3
) are

\displaystyle\hat{\K}_{[2]}(z,k)=\K_{3+2\epsilon,2+\epsilon}(z,k)

\displaystyle=z^{1+\epsilon}e^{-kz},

(10)

\displaystyle\hat{\K}_{[3]}(z,k)=\K_{3+2\epsilon,3+\epsilon}(z,k)

\displaystyle=z^{\epsilon}e^{-kz}(1+kz)

(11)

while the bulk-to-bulk propagators (
•
‣
2
) reduce to

\displaystyle\hat{\G}_{[2]}(z,k;\zeta)

\displaystyle=\G_{3+2\epsilon,2+\epsilon}(z,k;\zeta)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{(z\zeta)^{1+\epsilon}}{k}e^{-k\zeta}\sinh(kz)&\text{ for }z<\zeta,\\
\frac{(z\zeta)^{1+\epsilon}}{k}e^{-kz}\sinh(k\zeta)&\text{ for }z>\zeta,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\hat{\G}_{[3]}(z,k;\zeta)

\displaystyle=\G_{3+2\epsilon,3+\epsilon}(z,k;\zeta)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{(z\zeta)^{\epsilon}}{k^{3}}e^{-k\zeta}(1+k\zeta)\left(kz\cosh(kz)-\sinh(kz)\right)&\text{ for }z<\zeta,\\
\frac{(z\zeta)^{\epsilon}}{k^{3}}e^{-kz}(1+kz)\left(z\zeta\cosh(k\zeta)-\sinh(k\zeta)\right)&\text{ for }z>\zeta.\end{array}\right.

The radial integrals in the 3- and 4-point contact diagrams (
7
) and (
•
‣
2
) can then be evaluated in terms of gamma functions and rational functions.

For exchange diagrams, the inner integral in (
•
‣
2
) is expressible in terms of incomplete gamma functions and rational functions. Depending on which end of the integration is fixed, we have

\displaystyle\gamma(\alpha,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-t}t^{\alpha-1},

\displaystyle\Gamma(\alpha,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-t}t^{\alpha-1}.

(16)

To calculate the outer integral in (
•
‣
2
) it then suffices to use the following integrals,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,x^{\mu-1}e^{-\beta x}\gamma(\nu,\alpha x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\nu}\Gamma(\mu+\nu)}{\nu(\alpha+\beta)^{\mu+\nu}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(1,\mu+\nu;\nu+1;\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}\right),

(17)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,x^{\mu-1}e^{-\beta x}\Gamma(\nu,\alpha x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\nu}\Gamma(\mu+\nu)}{\mu(\alpha+\beta)^{\mu+\nu}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(1,\mu+\nu;\mu+1;\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\right).

(18)

Since for a specific amplitude the variables
\mu
and
\nu
depend on the regulator
\epsilon
, one must series expand the hypergeometric function with respect to its parameters. In all cases the parameters reduce to integer values plus terms of order
O(\epsilon)
allowing use of the excellent Mathematica package
HypExp

Huber:2005yg
.

2
-,

- and

6
), (

), all regulated in the half-integer scheme (

2-point amplitudes

The 2-point amplitudes are

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[22]}=-k,\qquad\qquad\qquad\hat{i}_{[33]}=k^{3},

(19)

where the normalization matches that of the holographic 2-point functions.
In the half-integer scheme these amplitudes are finite and independent of the regulator
\epsilon
.

3-point amplitudes

For later use, we will need the regulated 3-point amplitudes expanded to order
\epsilon
. Alternatively, we can simply keep the full solutions evaluated to all orders in the regulator:

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[222]}

\displaystyle=\Div_{1}(k_{t}),

(20)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[322]}

\displaystyle=-\Div_{1}(k_{t})\left[(k_{2}+k_{3})+\frac{\epsilon k_{t}}{1-\epsilon}\right],

(21)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[332]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Div_{1}(k_{t})\left[(k_{3}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})+\frac{\epsilon k_{t}}{(1-\epsilon)(2-\epsilon)}\left((3-\epsilon)k_{3}-(1-\epsilon)(k_{1}+k_{2})\right)\right],

(22)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[333]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\Div_{1}(k_{t})\Big[(k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3})+\frac{\epsilon k_{t}}{(1-\epsilon)(3-\epsilon)}\left((4-\epsilon)(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-(1-\epsilon)(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{1}k_{3}+k_{2}k_{3})\right)\Big].

(23)

Here and throughout, we use the conventions listed in appendix
A
. In particular,
\Div_{a}(k)=\Gamma(a\epsilon)k^{-a\epsilon}
as given in (
•
‣
A.1
) and
k_{t}
is the total 3-point momentum magnitude as defined in (
473
).
Note that all these 3-point amplitudes exhibit a single pole in the regulator.

Contact diagrams

The regulated contact 4-point amplitudes valid to all orders in
\epsilon
are

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[2222]}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\epsilon}{k_{T}}\Div_{2}(k_{T}),

(24)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[3222]}

\displaystyle=\Div_{2}(k_{T})\left[1+\frac{2\epsilon\,k_{1}}{k_{T}}\right],

(25)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[3322]}

\displaystyle=\Div_{2}(k_{T})\left[\sigma_{(1)12}-\frac{k_{T}}{1-2\epsilon}+\frac{2\epsilon\,\sigma_{(2)12}}{k_{T}}\right],

(26)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[3332]}

\displaystyle=\Div_{2}(k_{T})\left[\sigma_{(2)123}-\frac{k_{T}\sigma_{(1)123}}{1-2\epsilon}+\frac{k_{T}^{2}}{2(1-\epsilon)(1-2\epsilon)}+\frac{2\epsilon\,\sigma_{(3)123}}{k_{T}}\right],

(27)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[3333]}

\displaystyle=\Div_{2}(k_{T})\left[\sigma_{(3)1234}-\frac{k_{T}\sigma_{(2)1234}}{1-2\epsilon}+\frac{k_{T}^{3}}{(1-2\epsilon)(3-2\epsilon)}+\frac{2\epsilon\,\sigma_{(4)1234}}{k_{T}}\right],

(28)

where
k_{T}
is the total 4-point momentum magnitude (
473
) and
\sigma
denotes various symmetric polynomials defined in (
474
). Note that the amplitude
\hat{i}_{[2222]}
is finite,
\hat{i}_{[2222]}=\frac{1}{k_{T}}+O(\epsilon)
, while all the remaining amplitudes are linearly divergent.

Exchange diagrams

We split the results for exchange diagrams into two parts,

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=i^{\text{div}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]},

(29)

where
i^{\text{div}}
contains both the divergences and logarithmic terms, while the finite part
i^{\text{fin}}
contains the remainder of the amplitude. This split is non-unique, but is convenient for organizing the amplitude and will be particularly useful in the context of renormalization and change of scheme as we will discuss later.

Exchange diagrams with
\Delta_{x}=2

For
\Delta_{x}=2
, we find:

•

i_{[22,22x2]}
is finite,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[22,22x2]}

\displaystyle=0+O(\epsilon),

(30)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[22,22x2]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2s}\mathcal{D}^{(+)},

(31)

where
s
is the usual Mandelstam variable (
472
) and
\mathcal{D}^{(+)}
is defined in (
485
).

•

i_{[32,22x2]}
exhibits a double pole,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[32,22x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{34+})+\Div_{2}(l_{34+})+O(\epsilon),

(32)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[32,22x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{2}}{2s}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}^{(-)}+\frac{1}{4}\left[2-\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{l_{12+}}\right)\right],

(33)

where
\mathcal{D}^{(-)}
is defined in (
486
) and
l_{ij+}
is defined in (
482
).

•

i_{[33,22x2]}
exhibits a single pole,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[33,22x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{2}\Div_{2}(k_{T})+O(\epsilon),

(34)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[33,22x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-s^{2}}{4s}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}+\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{k_{T}}\right)+1\right]+\frac{7}{8}(k_{3}+k_{4}).

(35)

•

i_{[32,32x2]}
exhibits a double pole,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[32,32x2]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{2}+s}{2}\left(\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{12+})+6\Div_{2}(l_{12+})\right)-\frac{k_{4}+s}{2}\left(\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{34+})+6\Div_{2}(l_{34+})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(s-2k_{1})\Div_{2}(l_{12+})+(s-2k_{3})\Div_{2}(l_{34+})+O(\epsilon),

(36)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[32,32x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{2}-k_{2}k_{4}}{2s}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}+\frac{k_{2}-k_{4}}{2}\mathcal{D}^{(-)}+\frac{k_{2}+k_{4}+2s}{4}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{l_{34+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(k_{3}+k_{4})\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{34+}}\right)+(k_{1}+k_{2})\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{12+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-3k_{T}-\frac{k_{2}+k_{4}}{2}-3s.

(37)

•

i_{[32,33x2]}
exhibits a double pole,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[32,33x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}}{4}\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{34+})+\left[s(k_{3}+k_{4})-(k_{3}^{2}+k_{3}k_{4}+k_{4}^{2})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{5s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}}{4}\right]\Div_{2}(l_{34+})+O(\epsilon),

(38)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[32,33x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}}{4}\left[\frac{k_{2}}{s}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}+\mathcal{D}^{(-)}\right]-\frac{s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}}{8}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{l_{12+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4}\left[k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}+(2s-k_{3}-k_{4})(k_{3}+k_{4})\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{l_{12+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(s+k_{2})(k_{3}+k_{4})\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{12+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4}\left[k_{1}^{2}-(k_{4}+k_{3}-k_{2})^{2}+4s(k_{3}+k_{4})\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{k_{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{16}\left[17s^{2}+5k_{1}^{2}-4k_{1}k_{2}-9k_{2}^{2}+4(5s+k_{1}-k_{2})\sigma_{(1)34}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\left.-12\sigma_{(1)34}^{2}+8\sigma_{(2)34}\right],

(39)

where
\sigma_{(m)J}
is defined in (
474
).

•

i_{[33,33x2]}
exhibits a single pole,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[33,33x2]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{6}\Div_{2}(k_{T})+O(\epsilon),

(40)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[33,33x2]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(s^{2}-k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})(s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2})}{8s}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4}(s^{2}-k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})(k_{3}+k_{4})\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{k_{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4}(s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2})(k_{1}+k_{2})\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{k_{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{72}\left(18s^{2}\sigma_{(1)1234}-25\sigma_{(1)1234}^{3}+63\sigma_{(1)1234}\sigma_{(2)1234}-27\sigma_{(3)1234}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{s}{4}(k_{1}+k_{2})(k_{3}+k_{4}).

(41)

Exchange diagrams with
\Delta_{x}=3

For
\Delta_{x}=3
, we find:

is finite,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[22,22x3]}

\displaystyle=0+O(\epsilon),

(42)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[22,22x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{(k_{1}+k_{2})(k_{3}+k_{4})}{2s^{3}}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}-\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{k_{T}}\right)-\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{k_{T}}\right)+\frac{1}{s}.

(43)

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[32,22x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Div_{2}(l_{12+})+O(\epsilon),

(44)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[32,22x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{(s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})(k_{3}+k_{4})}{4s^{3}}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}}{2s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{12+}}\right)+\frac{(k_{1}-k_{2})(k_{3}+k_{4})}{2s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{34+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{3s+4(k_{1}-k_{2})}{8s}.

(45)

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{6}\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{34+})-\frac{6s+7(k_{3}+k_{4})}{9}\Div_{2}(l_{34+})+O(\epsilon),

(46)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[33,22x3]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{6}\left[\frac{k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}}{s^{3}}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}+\mathcal{D}^{(-)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{12}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{l_{34+}}\right)+\frac{s^{3}+k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}}{3s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{l_{34+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{k_{T}(k_{1}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2})}{3s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{34+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{3}\left[\frac{k_{1}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2}}{s}+(k_{1}+k_{2})+\frac{7}{3}s+\frac{43}{18}(k_{3}+k_{4})\right].

(47)

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[32,32x3]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{2}+k_{4}}{2}\Div_{2}(k_{T})+O(\epsilon),

(48)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[32,32x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{(s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})(s^{2}+k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2})}{8s^{3}}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{(s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})(k_{3}-k_{4})}{4s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{12+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{(s^{2}+k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2})(k_{1}-k_{2})}{4s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{34+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{8s}\left[2(k_{1}-k_{2})(k_{3}-k_{4})-2s(k_{1}+k_{3})-5s(k_{2}+k_{4})\right].

(49)

•

i_{[32,33x3]}
exhibits a double pole,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[32,33x3]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}}{12}\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{12+})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{36}\left[11s^{2}+12s(k_{1}-k_{2})+4(2k_{1}-5k_{2})(k_{1}+k_{2})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+9(k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2})\right]\Div_{2}(l_{12+})+O(\epsilon),

(50)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[32,33x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{(s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})}{12}\left[-\frac{k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{s^{3}}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}+\mathcal{D}^{(-)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}}{24}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{l_{34+}}\right)-\frac{(k_{1}-k_{2})(s^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3})}{6s^{2}}\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{l_{34+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{k_{T}}{12}\left[k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4}+\frac{2(k_{1}-k_{2})(k_{3}^{2}-k_{3}k_{4}+k_{4}^{2})}{s^{2}}\right]\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{l_{12+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{(k_{1}-k_{2})(k_{3}^{2}-k_{3}k_{4}+k_{4}^{2})}{6s}+\frac{1}{432}\left[-52k_{1}^{2}+292k_{2}^{2}+108k_{2}(k_{3}+k_{4})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-9(11k_{3}^{2}+4k_{3}k_{4}+11k_{4}^{2})+12k_{1}(20k_{2}-3k_{3}-3k_{4})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{s(11k_{1}-17k_{2})}{36}-\frac{85s^{2}}{432}.

(51)

•

i_{[33,33x3]}
exhibits a double pole,

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[33,33x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{3}+k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}}{18}\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{12+})+\frac{s^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{18}\Div_{1}^{2}(l_{34+})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{9}\left[3\sigma_{(1)12s}^{3}-7\sigma_{(1)12s}\sigma_{(2)12s}+\sigma_{(3)12s}-\frac{s^{3}}{3}\right]\Div_{2}(l_{12+})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{9}\left[3\sigma_{(1)34s}^{3}-7\sigma_{(1)34s}\sigma_{(2)34s}+\sigma_{(3)34s}-\frac{s^{3}}{3}\right]\Div_{2}(l_{34+})+O(\epsilon),

(52)

\displaystyle i^{\text{fin}}_{[33,33x3]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2s^{3}+k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{36}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{l_{34+}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{9}\left[s^{2}(k_{1}+k_{2})+s(k_{1}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2}-k_{3}^{2}+k_{3}k_{4}-k_{4}^{2})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{(k_{1}+k_{2})^{3}}{3}-\frac{(k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3})(k_{3}^{2}-k_{3}k_{4}+k_{4}^{2})}{s^{2}}\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{k_{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{9}\left[s^{2}(k_{3}+k_{4})+s(k_{3}^{2}-k_{3}k_{4}+k_{4}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}+k_{1}k_{2}-k_{2}^{2})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{(k_{3}+k_{4})^{3}}{3}-\frac{(k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3})(k_{1}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2})}{s^{2}}\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{k_{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{(k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3})(k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3})-s^{6}}{18s^{3}}\mathcal{D}^{(+)}+\frac{k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}-k_{1}^{3}-k_{2}^{3}}{18}\mathcal{D}^{(-)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{27}\Big[14s^{3}+11s^{2}k_{T}+s\left(8(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2})-5(k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{6}\left(61\sigma_{(1)1234}^{3}-129\sigma_{(1)1234}\sigma_{(2)1234}+15\sigma_{(3)1234}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+5\left(2\sigma_{(1)34}\sigma_{(2)12}+2\sigma_{(1)12}\sigma_{(2)34}-\sigma_{(1)12}\sigma_{(1)34}k_{T}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{3(k_{1}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2})(k_{3}^{2}-k_{3}k_{4}+k_{4}^{2})}{s}\Big].

(53)

OPE limit of exchange diagrams

An interesting limit of the
s
-channel exchange diagram is

s\rightarrow 0,\qquad k_{1}\approx k_{2},\qquad k_{3}\approx k_{4}

(54)

which probes configurations where
x_{12}^{2}\rightarrow 0
and
x_{34}^{2}\rightarrow 0
in position space
Arkani-Hamed:2015bza
;
Freedman:1998bj
;
Suyama:2007bg
;
Assassi:2012zq
. Applying the operator product expansion

\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{i}}(\bm{x}_{i})\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{j}}(\bm{x}_{j})\sim\frac{C_{ijx}}{x_{12}^{\Delta_{i}+\Delta_{j}-\Delta_{x}}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{x}}(\bm{x}_{i})+\ldots

(55)

to the pairs
(i,j)=(1,2)
and
(3,4)
yields the 4-point function

x_{12}^{\Delta_{x}-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}}x_{34}^{\Delta_{x}-\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4}}x_{13}^{-2\Delta_{x}}

which corresponds in momentum space to

i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}\sim k_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{x}-d}k_{3}^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}-\Delta_{x}-d}s^{2\Delta_{x}-d}.

(56)

Naively, one might think this argument gives the leading behaviour in the limit as
s\rightarrow 0
.
In fact, however, this expectation is only correct for cases where
\Delta_{x}<d/2
, and not for those studied here where
\Delta_{x}>d/2
. This in exact agreement with the results in
Bzowski:2014qja
where the same subtlety was discussed for 3-point functions.

To illustrate this, consider for example the OPE limit of the simplest exchange diagram (
30
), where all
\Delta_{j}=\Delta_{x}=2
.
This corresponds to evaluating

\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\Big[-\frac{1}{2s}\Big(\Li_{2}\Big(\frac{k_{1}-s/2}{k_{1}+k_{3}}\Big)+\Li_{2}\Big(\frac{k_{3}-s/2}{k_{1}+k_{3}}\Big)+\log\Big(\frac{k_{1}-s/2}{k_{1}+k_{3}}\Big)\log\Big(\frac{k_{3}-s/2}{k_{1}+k_{3}}\Big)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\Big)\Big].

(57)

The limit is of the form
0/0
since the numerator vanishes for
s=0

by the identity

0=\Li_{2}(x)+\Li_{2}(1-x)+\log x\log(1-x)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}

(58)

setting
x=k_{1}/(k_{1}+k_{3})
. Since
0<x<1
all logarithms and dilogarithms are evaluated on their principal branches.
Using l’Hôpital’s rule, we then find

i_{[22,22x2]}\rightarrow-\frac{1}{2k_{3}}\log\Big(\frac{k_{1}}{k_{1}+k_{3}}\Big)-\frac{1}{2k_{1}}\log\Big(\frac{k_{3}}{k_{1}+k_{3}}\Big)

(59)

in contradiction with (
56
).

The subleading nature of the OPE behaviour (
56
) for
\Delta_{x}>d/2

can clearly be seen from the
s\rightarrow 0
limit of the bulk-bulk propagator (
•
‣
2
). For
z<\zeta
we have

\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\G_{d,\Delta}(z,s;\zeta)=\frac{z^{\Delta_{x}}\zeta^{d-\Delta_{x}}}{2\Delta_{x}-d}\Big(1+O(s^{2})\Big)+s^{2\Delta_{x}-d}\Big(Cz^{\Delta_{x}}\zeta^{\Delta_{x}}+O(s^{2})\Big)

(60)

where
C=2^{d-2\Delta_{x}-1}\Gamma(d/2-\Delta_{x})/\Gamma(\Delta_{x}-d/2+1)
, and for
z>\zeta
the same expression holds with
z\leftrightarrow\zeta
. As
s\rightarrow 0
, therefore, the term proportional to
s^{2\Delta_{x}-d}
only dominates for
\Delta_{x}<d/2
. In this case, the leading behaviour is symmetric under interchanging
z\leftrightarrow\zeta
and the exchange diagram factorizes as

\displaystyle i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle\rightarrow Cs^{2\Delta_{x}-d}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{d+1}}z^{\Delta_{x}}\K_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\K_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{\zeta^{d+1}}\zeta^{\Delta_{x}}\K_{d,\Delta_{3}}(\zeta,k_{3})\K_{d,\Delta_{4}}(\zeta,k_{3})

\displaystyle\quad\sim s^{2\Delta_{x}-d}k_{1}^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{x}-d}k_{3}^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}-\Delta_{x}-d}

(61)

consistent with the OPE behaviour (
56
).
For
\Delta_{x}>d/2
, however, the leading behaviour of the bulk-bulk propagator corresponds instead to the first term in (
60
).
The
s
-dependence is then subleading invalidating the naive OPE expectation (
56
) as we saw above.
As the leading behaviour of the bulk-bulk propagator is no longer symmetric under
z\leftrightarrow\zeta
, we cannot simply factorize the exchange diagram into a product of 3-point functions as in (
3.5
). Instead, we have an inner
\zeta
-integral over
0\leq\zeta<z
using (
60
) with
z\leftrightarrow\zeta
, and an outer
\zeta
-integral over
z<\zeta<\infty
using (
60
),
giving rise to the result (
59
).

In conclusion, care must be taken when applying OPE arguments to ascertain the leading behaviour of momentum-space correlators.
The application of the OPE for understanding the conformal block structure of momentum-space correlators is an interesting open direction
(see,
e.g.,

Gillioz:2020wgw
for recent work).

Renormalization

While the amplitudes listed in the previous section are naturally associated with AdS Witten diagrams,
we do not necessarily have to invoke any form of holography. In principle, it is sufficient simply to write down the expressions (
6
)-(
•
‣
2
) and verify that they solve the regulated conformal Ward identities (CWIs).
This check is performed explicitly in the Mathematica notebook
RegulatedAmplitudes.nb
using the Ward identities listed in Appendix
B
.   Since every amplitude
satisfies the Ward identities, each potentially represents a valid correlation function.

However, the fact that a given expression satisfies the CWIs does not by itself imply that a CFT with such a 4-point function actually
exists. Other constraints, for example unitarity or the bootstrap, could rule out the existence of such a CFT. Nevertheless, we will show in Section
4.2
that, for each amplitude listed in the previous section, a holographic CFT can be constructed
for which the corresponding correlation function is equal to this amplitude,
i.e.
, a bulk action exists such that the Witten diagram is the entire correlator. If it turns out that a CFT with the desired spectrum and correlators does not exist, then the corresponding bulk theory would be in the swampland.

Next, we will discuss the renormalization of the regulated amplitudes listed in the previous section. In a textbook approach, it is usually the correlation functions themselves that are renormalized. However, as every regulated amplitude here is equal to a valid regulated correlator in some CFT, every amplitude must be individually renormalizable. This means, for example, that its divergences must be of an appropriately local form, so that eventually they can be removed by a suitable local counterterm. In particular, the divergences of an
n
-point function must take the form of lower-point functions of the same operators, plus possible additional ultralocal pieces. Our central focus will therefore be on renormalizing individual Witten diagrams. We will be able to write down counterterm actions and counterterm contributions that can be used in the renormalization procedure once the theory is specified. The renormalized amplitudes are listed in Section
4.3
.

As an illustration of the complete renormalization procedure, we will consider two holographic theories which we call
symmetric
and
asymmetric
. The asymmetric theory, defined in Section
4.2
, contains five operators of dimension two or three and is the least symmetric theory in the sense that there are no non-vanishing 4-point functions containing identical operators.
Even when some operators have the same dimension, the correlators are not symmetric under exchange of these operators as they are not identical.
The symmetric theory, on the other hand, contains only two operators, one of dimension two and one of dimension three, and there are non-vanishing 4-point function containing identical operators. These correlators are symmetric under exchange of the operators of the same dimension. The dual bulk theory contains bulk scalars corresponding to the boundary operators (five and two scalars, respectively) plus suitable
3
- and
4
-point interaction terms. In Sections
4.4
and
4.5
, we carry out the complete renormalization procedure for these two theories. For the symmetric theory, we further derive the beta functions and anomalies and verify the Callan-Symanzik equation.

From a holographic perspective, the renormalization procedure we adopt is a hybrid one: boundary correlation functions are derived from bulk amplitudes at generic dimensions
d
and
\Delta_{j}
, and the subsequent renormalization is performed entirely within the boundary theory. Thus, instead of regulating the bulk theory by cutting off the radial direction as per holographic renormalization, we employ dimensional regularization in the boundary theory as,
e.g.
, in
Schwimmer:2000cu
;
Schwimmer:2003eq
. The resulting counterterms are an integral part of the bulk theory (dimensionally regularized as in
Bzowski:2016kni
;
Bzowski:2019xri
) as the counterterms are also uniquely determined by requiring the bulk variational problem to be well-posed
Papadimitriou:2005ii
.

Conventions and definitions

In this section, we use the following conventions and definitions:

•

The counterterm contribution
i^{\text{ct}}
is added to the regulated amplitude
\hat{i}
, to yield the finite renormalized amplitude
i^{\text{ren}}

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left[\hat{i}+i^{\text{ct}}\right].

(62)

•

For the exchange amplitudes, we will list only their
logarithmic terms
defined by

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left[i^{\text{div}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+i^{\text{ct}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}\right].

(63)

The full amplitude then reads

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=i^{\text{log}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]},

(64)

where the finite parts
i^{\text{fin}}
are as listed in Sections
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
.

•

As previously, the Euclidean
\text{AdS}_{4}
metric in Poincaré coordinates is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}z^{2}+\mathrm{d}\bm{x}^{2}}{z^{2}}.

(65)

The AdS/CFT correspondence in the half-integer regularization scheme then gives

\displaystyle W[\phi_{j}]=-S_{\text{on-shell}}[\phi_{j}],

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle_{s,\text{reg}}=-(2\Delta-d)\phi_{(\Delta+\epsilon)}.

(66)

In particular, the combination
2\Delta-d
remains unchanged in the half-integer scheme.

For every operator
\mathcal{O}_{j}
, we denote its source by
\phi_{j}
. If the operator has dimension
\Delta_{j}
, its source has dimension
d-\Delta_{j}
and thus
\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[\Delta_{j}]}
couples to
\phi_{j}^{[d-\Delta_{j}]}
. In particular
\mathcal{O}^{[2]}
is sourced by
\phi^{[1]}
, while
\mathcal{O}^{[3]}
is sourced by
\phi^{[0]}
.

•

All counterterms appear with the renormalization scale
\mu
, and multiplied by constants
that we collectively denote as
\mathfrak{a}
. These constants are series in
\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}=1+\epsilon\,\mathfrak{a}^{(1)}+\epsilon^{2}\,\mathfrak{a}^{(2)}+O(\epsilon^{3}),

(67)

and the
\epsilon
-dependent part is generally scheme-dependent. As we show, one may choose a scheme such that the scheme-dependent part depends only on the dimensions of the operators under consideration, and not on any other labels of the operators. However, renormalisation conditions may require different choices of scheme-dependent constants for different operators.

Every amplitude is a correlator

Here we consider the holographic theory that realizes each amplitude (Witten diagram) as a stand-alone correlation function. We consider five scalar fields
\Phi_{j}
with
j=1,2,3,4,x
dual to operators
\mathcal{O}_{j}
of arbitrary dimensions
\Delta_{j}
. The (unregulated) bulk action, up to quartic order, is

\displaystyle S^{\text{asym}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{g}\sum_{j=1,2,3,4,x}\left[\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{j}\partial^{\mu}\Phi_{j}+m^{2}_{\Delta_{j}}\Phi_{j}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\int\mathrm{d}^{d}x\sqrt{g}\left[\lambda_{12x}\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{x}+\lambda_{34x}\Phi_{x}\Phi_{3}\Phi_{4}-\lambda_{1234}\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{3}\Phi_{4}\right],

(68)

where

\displaystyle m^{2}_{\Delta}=\Delta(\Delta-d)

(69)

and
\lambda_{12x},\lambda_{34x},\lambda_{1234}
are arbitrary AdS couplings. We refer to this theory as the
asymmetric theory
, since the resulting correlators have as few discrete symmetries as possible.
Later on, in Section
4.5
, we will introduce the
symmetric theory
where all correlators exhibit some form of crossing symmetry.

Regulated correlators

Let us now derive the regulated correlators of the asymmetric theory (
4.2
), following
Skenderis:2002wp
.

•

Since the action (
4.2
) is symmetric under the exchange of the fields
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
, their dynamics is identical. The same conclusion holds for
\Phi_{3}
and
\Phi_{4}
. The equations of motion for the bulk fields read

\displaystyle(-\Box_{AdS}+\hat{m}^{2}_{\Delta_{1}})\Phi_{1}=-\lambda_{12x}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{x}+\lambda_{1234}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{3}\Phi_{4},

(70)

\displaystyle(-\Box_{AdS}+\hat{m}^{2}_{\Delta_{3}})\Phi_{3}=-\lambda_{34x}\Phi_{4}\Phi_{x}+\lambda_{1234}\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\Phi_{4},

(71)

\displaystyle(-\Box_{AdS}+\hat{m}^{2}_{\Delta_{x}})\Phi_{x}=-\lambda_{12x}\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}-\lambda_{34x}\Phi_{3}\Phi_{4}.

(72)

•

The equations of motion can now be solved perturbatively to obtain terms with up to and including three sources. Let us denote by
\Phi_{n\{j\}}
the term in the solution depending on any
(j+1)
sources. Thus, for example,
\Phi_{n\{0\}}
is the solution to the free field equation. Concentrating on
\Phi_{1}
, in position space we find

\displaystyle\Phi_{1\{0\}}

\displaystyle=\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast\phi_{1},

(73)

\displaystyle\Phi_{1\{1\}}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{12x}\G_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast(\Phi_{2\{0\}}\Phi_{x\{0\}}),

(74)

\displaystyle\Phi_{1\{2\}}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{12x}\G_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast\left[\Phi_{2\{1\}}\Phi_{x\{0\}}+\Phi_{2\{0\}}\Phi_{x\{1\}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{1234}\G_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast(\Phi_{2\{0\}}\Phi_{3\{0\}}\Phi_{4\{0\}}),

(75)

where
\ast
indicates the convolution of the position-space variables,
3
3

3

In (
73
) the integral is taken only over the boundary directions.

e.g.,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1\{1\}}(x)=-\lambda_{12x}\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x^{\prime}\,\sqrt{g(x^{\prime})}\G_{[\Delta_{1}]}(x,x^{\prime})\Phi_{2\{0\}}(x^{\prime})\Phi_{x\{0\}}(x^{\prime}).

(76)

•

The holographic 2-point functions are diagonal and given by the standard expression

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{j}(\bm{k})\mathcal{O}_{j}(-\bm{k})\rangle\!\rangle=(2\Delta-d)\times\text{ coefficient of }z^{\Delta}\text{ in }\K_{\Delta}(z,k).

(77)

In Section
3.1
, we defined the 2-point amplitudes
i_{[\Delta\Delta]}
in (
6
) in such a way that they are equal to the holographic 2-point function,

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{j}(\bm{k})\mathcal{O}_{j}(-\bm{k})\rangle\!\rangle=i_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}.

(78)

•

To go to the boundary for higher-point functions, we use the fact that

\displaystyle\G_{[\Delta]}(z,k;\zeta)=\frac{z^{\Delta}}{2\Delta-d}\K_{[\Delta]}(\zeta,k)+O(z^{\Delta+2}).

(79)

Thus, from (
66
) we see that regulated 1-point functions with sources reads

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\rangle_{\{1\}s,\text{reg}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{12x}\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast(\K_{[\Delta_{2}]}\ast\phi_{2})(\K_{[\Delta_{x}]}\ast\phi_{x}),

(80)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\rangle_{\{2\}s,\text{reg}}

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{12x}\lambda_{34x}\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast\left[(\K_{[\Delta_{2}]}\ast\phi_{2})\G_{[\Delta_{x}]}\ast(\K_{[\Delta_{3}]}\ast\phi_{3})(\K_{[\Delta_{4}]}\ast\phi_{4})\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\lambda_{12x}^{2}\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast\left[(\K_{[\Delta_{x}]}\ast\phi_{x})\G_{[\Delta_{2}]}\ast(\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast\phi_{1})(\K_{[\Delta_{x}]}\ast\phi_{x})\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\lambda_{1234}\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}\ast(\K_{[\Delta_{2}]}\ast\phi_{2})(\K_{[\Delta_{3}]}\ast\phi_{3})(\K_{[\Delta_{4}]}\ast\phi_{4}).

(81)

The
(n+1)
-point function is then obtained by taking
n
functional derivatives with the factor
(-1)^{n}
.

These expressions represent the 3- and 4-point Witten diagrams, but with sources present. For example, (
80
) written out explicitly is

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(x_{1})\rangle_{\{1\}s,\text{reg}}=\lambda_{12x}\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x\,\sqrt{g(x)}\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}(x_{1},x)\times

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x_{2}\,\sqrt{g(x_{2})}\K_{[\Delta_{2}]}(x,x_{2})\phi_{2}(x_{2})\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x_{3}\,\sqrt{g(x_{3})}\K_{[\Delta_{x}]}(x,x_{3})\phi_{x}(x_{3})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{12x}\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x_{2}\sqrt{g(x_{2})}\int\mathrm{d}^{d+1}x_{3}\sqrt{g(x_{3})}\,i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\phi_{2}(x_{2})\phi_{x}(x_{3}),

(82)

where here
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
denotes the position space expression for the 3-point amplitude
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
. We will not work in position space any further in this paper.

\mathcal{O}_{1}(x_{1})
\phi_{3}(x_{3})
\phi_{2}(x_{2})
\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}
\K_{[\Delta_{x}]}
\K_{[\Delta_{2}]}
\lambda_{12x}
\mathcal{O}_{1}(x_{1})
\phi_{2}(x_{2})
\phi_{3}(x_{3})
\phi_{4}(x_{4})
\K_{[\Delta_{1}]}
\K_{[\Delta_{2}]}
\K_{[\Delta_{3}]}
\K_{[\Delta_{4}]}
\G_{[\Delta_{x}]}
\lambda_{34x}
\lambda_{12x}

Figure 3:
3- and 4-point Witten diagrams corresponding to (
80
) and the first line of (
81
).

•

Directly from the action (
4.2
), we see that there are two non-vanishing 3-point functions,
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
. From (
80
), we see that

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{i}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{j}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{k}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda_{ijx}\,i_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j}\Delta_{x}]}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

(83)

where
(ij)=(12)
or
(34)
.

•

Among the non-vanishing 4-point functions, the most important is
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
. This contains two contributions: a single exchange diagram and a single contact diagram. From (
81
),

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{k}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{k}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\lambda_{12x}\lambda_{34x}\,i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+\lambda_{1234}\,i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}.

(84)

If we want a bulk theory where the contact diagram
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
is a correlator on its own, it therefore suffices to consider a bulk action with
\lambda_{12x}=0
or
\lambda_{34x}=0
. On the other hand, if we want the exchange diagram to be a correlator on its own, then we need
\lambda_{1234}=0
.

Note that
i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
and

i_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
only depend on the dimensions on the operators involved – not on any other labels that the operators may have, or whether the operators are identical or not – and so we have just demonstrated that each is on its own a correlator in a specific bulk theory.

•

For completeness, we note that the asymmetric theory has additional non-vanishing 4-point functions:

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{i}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{j}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{j}(\bm{k}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{i}(\bm{k}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\lambda_{ijk}^{2}\,\left[i_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j},\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i}x\Delta_{k}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2};\bm{k}_{3},\bm{k}_{4})+i_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j},\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i}x\Delta_{k}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{3};\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{4})\right],

(85)

where
(ijk)=(12x)
or
(34x)
or any permutation within each bracket (see Figure
4
on page
4
).

In conclusion, all
3
- and
4
-point Witten diagrams are valid
3
- and
4
-point functions as they can be realized in the holographic theory given by (
4.2
) for specific choices for couplings. Additional symmetry factors may appear if correlation functions of the same operator are considered. Similar conclusions can be reached for all higher-point diagrams.

Renormalized amplitudes

3-point functions

Since every amplitude is a correlator, we can now list the counterterm actions that renormalize the
3
- and
4
-point functions in (
83
) - (
•
‣
4.2.1
). We set
\lambda_{12x}=\lambda_{34x}=\lambda_{1234}=1
, so the counterterm contributions derived from the actions cure the divergences of the amplitudes only. We will restore the
\lambda
dependence when we discuss specific bulk actions. Let us start with the 3-point function to give some more details on the procedure.

•

The form of the counterterms depends on the dimensions of the operators involved, and read

\displaystyle S_{[222]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[222]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[1]}_{1}\phi^{[1]}_{2}\phi^{[1]}_{3},

(86)

\displaystyle S_{[322]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[0]}_{1}(\phi^{[1]}_{2}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{3}+\phi^{[1]}_{3}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{2}),

(87)

\displaystyle S_{[332]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[332]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[1]}_{3}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\partial^{\mu}\phi^{[0]}_{2},

(88)

\displaystyle S_{[333]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[333]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,(\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{3}+\phi^{[0]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{1}+\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{2}),

(89)

where the values of the constants are

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[222]}=-\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[222]},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}=\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[322]},

(90)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[332]}=\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[332]},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[333]}=\frac{1}{3}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}.

(91)

The scheme-dependent part of the constants
\mathfrak{a}
could in principle be different for different operators, but one may choose to work in a scheme where they depend only on the dimensions of the operators involved and are the same regardless of the permutation of the dimensions,
e.g.
,
\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}=\mathfrak{a}_{[232]}=\mathfrak{a}_{[223]}
. In all cases, the constants have an
\epsilon
-expansion given by (
67
).

•

Given a counterterm action
S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}^{\text{ct}\>(3)}
, its contribution
i^{\text{ct}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
to the 3-point amplitude in (
83
) is obtained by taking three functional derivatives, with respect to the sources, of the generating functional incorporating the counterterm action:

\displaystyle\frac{-\delta^{3}}{\delta\phi_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\delta\phi_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\delta\phi_{3}(\bm{x}_{3})}\langle\exp\left(-S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}^{\text{ct}\>(3)}\right)\rangle_{s,\text{reg}}=\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}+i^{\text{ct}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}.

(92)

We view
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
as following from the differentiation of the regulated generating functional. The expression on the right-hand side should now possesses a finite limit as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

•

From the counterterm actions above, the counterterm contributions to the 3-point amplitudes are

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}}_{[222]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[222]},

(93)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}}_{[322]}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}\times\left(\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{2})+\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{3})\right),

(94)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}}_{[332]}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{s}_{[332]}\times(\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2}),

(95)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}}_{[333]}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{s}_{[333]}\times\left(\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{1})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{2})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{3})\right).

(96)

It is easy to see they match perfectly the divergences in (
20
) - (
23
).

•

A brief comment on notation is in order.
The form of the counterterms (
86
) - (
89
) depends on the dimensions of the operators involved, as indicated by the subscript in square brackets. Here, we assume that all three operators and their sources featuring in the counterterms are distinct, as indicated by the indices. In particular, the counterterms functionally depend on the sources and operators, so more accurately, listing explicitly the functional dependence, we have

\displaystyle S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}

\displaystyle=S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}(\phi_{1}^{[3-\Delta_{1}]},\phi_{2}^{[3-\Delta_{2}]},\phi_{3}^{[3-\Delta_{3}]};\mathcal{O}_{1}^{[\Delta_{1}]},\mathcal{O}_{2}^{[\Delta_{2}]},\mathcal{O}_{3}^{[\Delta_{3}]})

\displaystyle=S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3}).

(97)

We will not list the arguments explicitly however unless some of them coincide. We will also drop the operators and dimensions, as shown in the second line, assuming that the pairing of the sources and operators is fixed. Note that the conformal dimensions in
S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}
cannot be dropped, as they indicate which form of the counterterm we consider.

If two or more fields coincide, additional symmetry factors may be required. If
n
sources in a given counterterm coincide, its counterterm contribution equals
n!\,i^{\text{ct}}
. For example,
S_{[222]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3})
produces
i^{\text{ct}}_{[222]}
when the three sources are different,
S_{[222]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}(\phi_{1},\phi_{1},\phi_{3})
produces
2i^{\text{ct}}_{[222]}
, while with the identical sources
S_{[222]}^{\text{ct}\,(3)}(\phi,\phi,\phi)
gives
6i^{\text{ct}}_{[222]}
. Note also that some counterterms contain several terms, which would coincide when some sources are equal.

4-point functions

In order to write the remaining counterterms for
4
-point functions, we notice two facts. First, the
3
-point counterterms (
86
) - (
89
) will generally contribute to 4-point amplitudes, and this contribution must be taken into account before the 4-point counterterm is added. Second, the form of the 4-point counterterms cannot depend on the operator in the exchange channel, because the counterterm action can only depend on the four external sources and/or operators,
\phi_{j}
and
\mathcal{O}_{j}
for
j=1,2,3,4
.

•

It is convenient to list first all the types of counterterms contributing to the 4-point amplitudes of interest, as given in Table

.

Singularity and counterterm types for various 4-point amplitudes. The first column specifies the type of amplitude: only the dimensions of the external operators as listed are relevant.

Starting with the counterterm actions (
86
) - (

), one can take four functional derivatives similar to (

) and obtain their contributions to the 4-point amplitudes. For
\Delta_{x}=2

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[22,22x2]}

\displaystyle=0,

(98)

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}\hat{i}_{[222]}(s,k_{3},k_{4}),

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[33,22x2]}

\displaystyle=0,

(100)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[32,32x2]}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}\left[\hat{i}_{[322]}(k_{1},k_{2},s)+\hat{i}_{[322]}(k_{3},k_{4},s)\right]+\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}^{2}\hat{i}_{[22]}(s),

(101)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[32,33x2]}

(102)

\displaystyle=0

(103)

and for
\Delta_{x}=3
one finds,

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[22,22x3]}

\displaystyle=0,

(104)

\displaystyle=0,

(105)

(106)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[32,32x3]}

\displaystyle=0,

(107)

(109)

•

We can now write down the most general local terms consistent with the symmetries that are not fixed by the renormalization of the 3-point functions. These are

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[22,22x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=0,

(110)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[1]}_{2}\phi^{[1]}_{3}\phi^{[1]}_{4},

(111)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\left(\phi^{[1]}_{3}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{4}+\phi^{[1]}_{4}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{3}\right),

(112)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\left(\phi^{[1]}_{2}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{4}+\phi^{[1]}_{4}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{2}\right),

(113)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\left[\mathfrak{s}^{\{3+4\}}_{[33,23x\Delta_{x}]}\left(\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[1]}_{2}(\partial^{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3})\,\phi^{[0]}_{4}+\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[1]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}(\partial^{2}\phi^{[0]}_{4})\right)\right.

(114)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathfrak{s}^{\{1\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}(\partial^{2}\phi^{[0]}_{1})\,\phi^{[1]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}+\mathfrak{s}^{\{2\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}\phi^{[0]}_{1}(\partial^{2}\phi^{[1]}_{2})\,\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+\mathfrak{s}^{\{34\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[1]}_{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\partial^{\mu}\phi^{[0]}_{4}+\mathfrak{s}^{\{12\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}\partial_{\mu}(\partial^{\mu}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\,\phi^{[1]}_{2})\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}\right],

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[33,33x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[33,33x\Delta_{x}]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\left(\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{4}+\phi^{[0]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{1}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{2}+\phi^{[0]}_{4}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{3}\right),

(115)

where the labels in curly brackets in
\mathfrak{s}^{\{\ldots\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}
indicate on which sources the
\partial^{2}
acts. For example,
\{3+4\}
indicates it acts on
\phi^{[0]}_{3}
and
\phi^{[0]}_{4}
,
\{1\}
that it acts on
\phi^{[0]}_{1}
,

\{34\}
in the product
\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}
and so on, with the precise structure as listed above.
Note the representation (
114
) is over-complete: only four of the terms listed are independent, and the fifth can always be obtained through integration by parts. In particular, we can always set one of the four constants
\mathfrak{s}^{\{\ldots\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}
to zero. We will retain this over-complete representation, however, as it will prove convenient to use different non-vanishing structures for
\Delta_{x}=2
and
\Delta_{x}=3
.

•

The contributions from the counterterm actions (
110
) - (
115
) are

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[22,22x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=0,

(116)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]},

(117)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}\left[\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{3})+\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{4})\right],

(118)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}\left[\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{2})+\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{4})\right],

(119)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}^{\{1\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}k_{1}^{2}+\mathfrak{s}^{\{2\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}k_{2}^{2}+\mathfrak{s}^{\{3+4\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}(k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{\{12\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}(s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{\{34\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}(s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}),

(120)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[33,33x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[33,33x\Delta_{x}]}\left[\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{1})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{2})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{3})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{4})\right],

(121)

where the values of the constants
\mathfrak{s}
are listed in Table
3
.

\Delta_{x}=2

\Delta_{x}=3

\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}

-\tfrac{1}{2}\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x2]}

\tfrac{1}{2}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x3]}

\mathfrak{s}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}

\tfrac{1}{2}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[33,22x2]}

\tfrac{1}{6}\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[33,22x3]}

\mathfrak{s}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}

\tfrac{1}{2}\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,32x2]}

-\tfrac{1}{2}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,32x3]}

\mathfrak{s}^{\{12\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}

0

-\tfrac{1}{6}\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{12\}}

\mathfrak{s}^{\{34\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}

\tfrac{1}{2}\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x2]}^{\{34\}}

0

\mathfrak{s}^{\{3+4\}}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}

\tfrac{3}{4}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x2]}^{\{3+4\}}

\tfrac{1}{4}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{3+4\}}

\tfrac{1}{12}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{2\}}

\mathfrak{s}_{[33,33x\Delta_{x}]}
