\tfrac{1}{18}\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[33,33x3]}

Table 3:
The values of the constants
\mathfrak{s}
for exchange diagrams with exchange
\Delta_{x}=2
and
\Delta_{x}=3
.

•

If the degree of divergence is higher than one, the subleading terms in the
\mathfrak{a}
constants are related to the 3-point
\mathfrak{a}
constants as explained in the following subsection.

This leads to the following set of identities, which it is important to impose in all subsequent calculations:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x2]}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}-1,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{[33,22x3]}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}-\tfrac{1}{3},

(122)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{[32,32x2]}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}-1,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{12\}(1)}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}-\tfrac{5}{6},

(123)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x2]}^{\{34\}(1)}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}+\tfrac{1}{2},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{[33,33x3]}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=2\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}-\tfrac{1}{3}.

(124)

Example

To understand this last point better, and as an example calculation, consider the 4-point amplitude
\hat{i}_{[32,22x2]}
. This amplitude exhibits a double pole at
\epsilon=0
and its divergent terms are given by (
32
). While one can work with the divergences expressed in terms of
\Div_{1}
and
\Div_{2}
, it is easier to understand the situation when fully expanded in the regulator,

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[32,22x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left[\log l_{34+}+\gamma_{E}-\tfrac{1}{2}\right]+O(\epsilon^{0}).

(125)

The renormalization of the 3-point amplitude
\hat{i}_{[322]}
introduces the counterterm (
87
), which also contributes to the 4-point amplitude under consideration. Its contribution is given by (
99
). With the value of
\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}
given in (
90
), we find

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[32,22x2]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left[\log l_{34+}+\log\mu+2\gamma_{E}-\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]+O(\epsilon^{0}).

(126)

Before moving to the renormalization of the 4-point functions, the renormalization procedure for 3-point functions must be carried out. This means that we treat
\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}
as fixed at this point.

To fully renormalize the 4-point amplitude, we have to introduce the counterterm (
111
). Its contribution to the 4-point function is then given by taking four functional derivatives with respect to the sources, with the result given by (
117
). Thus, the sum of (
117
), (
125
) and (
126
) must be finite. With
\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(n)}
denoting terms of order
\epsilon^{n}
in the expansion of
\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}
, we find

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[32,22x2]}+i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[32,22x2]}+i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[32,22x2]}=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\left[\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-2)}-\frac{1}{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left[\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-1)}+\gamma_{E}+\log\mu-\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{2}\right]+O(\epsilon^{0}).

(127)

Thus, we must choose

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-1)}

\displaystyle=-\gamma_{E}-\log\mu+\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{2}.

(128)

As we can see, both the leading
\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-2)}
as well as the subleading
\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-1)}
term is completely fixed by the renormalization. Furthermore, the subleading term
\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-1)}
depends on the subleading part of the renormalization constant
\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}
. Only the third term,
\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(0)}
, is unrestricted and will contribute to the finite part of the renormalized correlator.

In the discussion above we presented our results using the divergences
\Div_{1}
and
\Div_{2}
. These objects also keep track of the scale-dependence, so that the
\log\mu
term is automatically taken care of. For example, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-2)}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-1)}}{\epsilon}+O(\epsilon^{0})=\frac{1}{2}\left[1+\epsilon(2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}-1)\right]\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)+O(\epsilon^{0}).

(129)

Thus, if we identify the term in square brackets with the counterterm constant
\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x2]}=1+\epsilon\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x2]}^{(1)}+O(\epsilon^{2})
, we must impose
\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x2]}^{(1)}=2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}-1
. This is indeed the first relation in (
122
).

Finally, let us stress here that the relations (
122
) - (
124
) are essential for the theory to be renormalizable. Indeed, if
\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x2]}^{(-1)}
were any different in the example above, the 4-point amplitude
\hat{i}_{[32,22x2]}
could not be renormalized.

Contact diagrams

•

The form of the counterterms depends on the exchange operator only through the constants
\mathfrak{s}
. This also includes the contact diagrams, which we can identify with exchange diagrams with
\Delta_{x}=0
, but for the sake of clarity, let us rewrite these expressions,

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[2222]}

\displaystyle=0,

(130)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[3222]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[3222]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[1]}_{2}\phi^{[1]}_{3}\phi^{[1]}_{4},

(131)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[3322]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[3322]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\left(\phi^{[1]}_{3}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{4}+\phi^{[1]}_{4}\mathcal{O}^{[2]}_{3}\right),

(132)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[3332]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[3332]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\left(\partial^{2}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\,\phi^{[0]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[1]}_{4}+\phi^{[0]}_{1}\partial^{2}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\,\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[1]}_{4}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\partial^{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\,\phi^{[1]}_{4}-\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\partial^{2}\phi^{[1]}_{4}\right),

(133)

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[3333]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[3333]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\left(\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{4}+\phi^{[0]}_{2}\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{1}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\phi^{[0]}_{3}\phi^{[0]}_{4}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{2}+\phi^{[0]}_{4}\phi^{[0]}_{1}\phi^{[0]}_{2}\mathcal{O}^{[3]}_{3}\right).

(134)

The values of the constants are

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[3222]}

\displaystyle=\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[3222]},

(135)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[3322]}

\displaystyle=-\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[3322]},

(136)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[3332]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[3332]},

(137)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{[3333]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\Div_{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[3333]},

(138)

and the counterterm contributions are

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[2222]}

\displaystyle=0,

(139)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[3222]}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{s}_{[3222]},

(140)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[3322]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[3322]}\left[\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{3})+\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{4})\right],

(141)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[3332]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[3332]}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}),

(142)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[3333]}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{s}_{[3333]}\left[\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{1})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{2})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{3})+\hat{i}_{[33]}(k_{4})\right].

(143)

•

Similarly to the case of the 3-point counterterms in (
•
‣
4.3.1
), the 4-point counterterms depend functionally on four sources and operators,

\displaystyle S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}^{\text{ct}\,(4)}=S_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}^{\text{ct}\,(4)}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4}).

(144)

We omit the explicit arguments, if all the sources and operators are as listed.

•

In the remaining subsections, we will present two examples of how to use these results in practice.

Renormalized amplitudes

When the counterterm contributions
i^{\text{ct}}
are added to the regulated amplitudes
\hat{i}
, the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
exists. Here we list the
renormalized amplitudes

i^{\text{ren}}
defined as

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left[\hat{i}+i^{\text{ct}}\right].

(145)

•

2-point amplitudes are finite and do not require renormalization,

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[22]}

\displaystyle=-k,

(146)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[33]}

\displaystyle=k^{3},

(147)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[23]}

\displaystyle=0.

(148)

We do not allow for a counterterm of the form
\int\phi^{[0]}\Box\phi^{[1]}
, which would result in a local contribution to
\langle\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{[3]}\rangle
. We keep the 2-point functions diagonal.

•

The renormalized 3-point amplitudes are

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[222]}

\displaystyle=-\log\left(\frac{k_{t}}{\mu}\right)-\mathfrak{a}_{[222]}^{(1)},

(149)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[322]}

\displaystyle=(k_{2}+k_{3})\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{t}}{\mu}\right)+\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}-1\right]-k_{1},

(150)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[332]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-k_{3}^{2}}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{t}}{\mu}\right)+\mathfrak{a}_{[332]}^{(1)}-\tfrac{3}{2}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}(k_{1}+k_{2})\right],

(151)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[333]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}}{3}\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{t}}{\mu}\right)+\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}-\tfrac{4}{3}\right]+\frac{1}{3}\left[k_{1}^{2}k_{2}+5\text{ perms.}-k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}\right].

(152)

•

The renormalized contact diagrams are

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[2222]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{T}},

(153)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[3222]}

\displaystyle=-\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[3222]}^{(1)}\right]+\frac{k_{1}}{k_{T}},

(154)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[3322]}

\displaystyle=(k_{3}+k_{4})\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[3322]}^{(1)}\right]+\frac{k_{1}k_{2}}{k_{T}}-k_{T},

(155)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[3332]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[3332]}^{(1)}\right]+\frac{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}{k_{T}}+k_{T}\left(k_{4}-\frac{k_{T}}{4}\right),

(156)

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[3333]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{3}\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[3333]}^{(1)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}{k_{T}}+k_{T}\left(-\sigma_{(2)1234}+\frac{4}{9}k_{T}^{2}\right).

(157)

•

For exchange diagrams we only list the
logarithmic part
here,
i^{\text{log}}
, defined as

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\left[i^{\text{div}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+i^{\text{ct}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}\right].

(158)

The full renormalized amplitude then reads

\displaystyle i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=i^{\text{log}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+i^{\text{fin}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]},

(159)

where the finite parts
i^{\text{fin}}
are listed in Section
3
.

•

All in all, the logarithmic parts of the renormalized amplitudes with
\Delta_{x}=2
are

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[22,22x2]}

\displaystyle=0,

(160)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[32,22x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)+\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)\left[-1+\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]+\left[-\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(2)}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x2]}^{(2)}\right],

(161)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[33,22x2]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[33,22x2]}^{(1)}\right],

(162)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[32,32x2]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{2}+s}{2}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{\mu}\right)-\frac{k_{4}+s}{2}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left[(l_{12+}+k_{2})-(k_{2}+s)\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left[(l_{34+}+k_{4})-(k_{4}+s)\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(2s+k_{T})\left[1+\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]+(k_{2}+k_{4})\left[\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,32x2]}^{(2)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-s(\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)})^{2},

(163)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[32,33x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}}{4}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4}\left[-k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}+(3-2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)})(k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2})+2k_{3}k_{4}-2s(k_{3}+k_{4})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+2s^{2}(-1+\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)})\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{(k_{3}+k_{4})^{2}}{8}\left[1+2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]-\frac{s(k_{3}+k_{4})}{4}\left[3+2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{s^{2}}{4}\left[\frac{7}{2}+3\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right]+\frac{s^{2}-k_{3}^{2}-k_{4}^{2}}{4}\left[-2\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(2)}+\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x2]}^{\{34\}(2)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{8}\left[-k_{1}^{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x2]}^{\{1\}(1)}+k_{2}^{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x2]}^{\{2\}(1)}+3(k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2})\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x2]}^{\{3+4\}(1)}\right],

(164)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[33,33x2]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{6}\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[33,33x2]}^{(1)}\right].

(165)

•

The logarithmic parts of the renormalized amplitudes with
\Delta_{x}=3
are

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[22,22x3]}

\displaystyle=0,

(166)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[32,22x3]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,22x3]}^{(1)}\right],

(167)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[33,22x3]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{6}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{3}\left[s+\left(\frac{4}{3}-\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right)(k_{3}+k_{4})\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{3}\left[1+\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}+\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[33,22x3]}^{(2)}\right]+\frac{s}{3}\left[1+\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right],

(168)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[32,32x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{2}+k_{4}}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{k_{T}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,32x3]}^{(1)}\right],

(169)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[32,33x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{2}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}-s^{2}}{12}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{1}{6}\left[k_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{5}{6}-\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right)+k_{2}^{2}\left(-\frac{11}{6}+\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-k_{1}k_{2}+s(k_{1}-k_{2})+\frac{3}{2}(k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2})+s^{2}\left(\frac{4}{3}-\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right)\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{6}\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right)k_{2}(k_{1}+s)+\frac{1}{12}(1+2\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})sk_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2}}{8}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{3+4\}(1)}+\frac{s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}}{24}\left[1+2\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}+4\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(2)}-2\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{12\}(2)}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{k_{1}^{2}}{24}\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{1\}(1)}-\frac{k_{2}^{2}}{24}\left[6+4\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}-\mathfrak{a}_{[32,33x3]}^{\{2\}(1)}\right],

(170)

\displaystyle i^{\text{log}}_{[33,33x3]}

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{3}+k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}}{18}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{\mu}\right)+\frac{s^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{18}\log^{2}\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{1}{9}\left[\left(\frac{5}{3}-\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right)\sigma_{(1)12s}^{3}+(-4+3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})\sigma_{(1)12s}\sigma_{(2)12s}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\qquad\qquad\left.+(1-3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})\sigma_{(3)12s}-\frac{s^{3}}{3}\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{12+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{1}{9}\left[\left(\frac{5}{3}-\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right)\sigma_{(1)34s}^{3}+(-4+3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})\sigma_{(1)34s}\sigma_{(2)34s}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\qquad\qquad\left.+(1-3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})\sigma_{(3)34s}-\frac{s^{3}}{3}\right]\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}}{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{4+3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}}{27}\left[k_{1}k_{2}(k_{1}+k_{2})+k_{3}k_{4}(k_{3}+k_{4})+s(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}+k_{3}^{2}+k_{4}^{2})+s^{2}k_{T}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{s}{27}\left[1+3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}\right](k_{1}k_{2}+k_{3}k_{4})-\frac{s^{3}}{81}\left[26+24\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}-9(\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{26+24\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)}+18\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(2)}-9\mathfrak{a}_{[33,33x3]}^{(2)}}{162}(k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}+k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}).

(171)

Asymmetric theory

In the previous sections we listed: (
i
) the counterterm contributions to the amplitudes that would render them finite, (
ii
) the renormalized amplitudes, and (
iii
) the counterterm actions that produce the counterterm contributions. In this section, we apply these results to completely renormalize 2-, 3-, and 4-point functions in the asymmetric theory (
4.2
).

The 2-point functions
are finite and do not require renormalization. Thus, they already represent the renormalized correlators,

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[\Delta_{j}]}(\bm{k})\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[\Delta_{j}]}(-\bm{k})\rangle\!\rangle_{\text{ren}}

\displaystyle=i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]},

(172)

with
i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{j}\Delta_{j}]}
given by (
146
) and (
147
).

We will now show that the following CFT counterterm action renormalizes all 3- and 4-point functions in the asymmetric theory,

\displaystyle S^{\text{asym, ct}}=S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}+S_{1234}^{\text{asym, ct}\,(4)}+S_{\text{cross}}^{\text{asym, ct}\,(4)},

(173)

where

\displaystyle S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{12x}S^{\text{ct}\,(3)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{x})+\lambda_{34x}S^{\text{ct}\,(3)}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}(\phi_{3},\phi_{4},\phi_{x}),

(174)

\displaystyle S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(4)}_{1234}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{12x}\lambda_{34x}S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{1234}S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4}),

(175)

\displaystyle S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(4)}_{\text{cross}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{12x}^{2}\left[S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{2}\Delta_{1}x\Delta_{x}]}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{2},\phi_{1})+S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{x},\Delta_{x}\Delta_{1}x\Delta_{2}]}(\phi_{1},\phi_{x},\phi_{x},\phi_{1})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x},\Delta_{x}\Delta_{2}x\Delta_{1}]}(\phi_{2},\phi_{x},\phi_{x},\phi_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{34x}^{2}\left[S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4},\Delta_{4}\Delta_{3}x\Delta_{x}]}(\phi_{3},\phi_{4},\phi_{4},\phi_{3})+S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{x},\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}x\Delta_{4}]}(\phi_{3},\phi_{x},\phi_{x},\phi_{3})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x},\Delta_{x}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{3}]}(\phi_{4},\phi_{x},\phi_{x},\phi_{4})\right].

(176)

The counterterm actions in these expressions are listed in Section
4.3
.

The first term in (
173
),
S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}
, renormalizes the 3-point functions (
83
). Indeed, it contributes
\lambda_{12x}i^{\text{ct}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}
to the correlator
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x}\rangle
and
\lambda_{34x}i^{\text{ct}}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}
to the correlator
\langle\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{x}\rangle
. It also contributes to the 4-point functions, contributing
\lambda_{12x}\lambda_{34x}i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
to
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
. To see this more explicitly, consider an example with
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{x}=3
and
\Delta_{3}=\Delta_{4}=2
. In such a case
S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}
reads

\displaystyle S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}

\displaystyle=\Div_{1}(\mu)\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\left[\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{12x}\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}\left(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x}+\phi_{2}\phi_{x}\mathcal{O}_{1}+\phi_{x}\phi_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\lambda_{34x}\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}\phi_{x}\left(\phi_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}+\phi_{4}\mathcal{O}_{3}\right)\vphantom{\Big[}\right].

(177)

If we are interested in its contribution to the 4-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
, we can drop all sources
\phi_{x}
of the exchange scalar leaving only the first term. Then, we differentiate

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta^{4}}{\delta\phi_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\delta\phi_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\delta\phi_{3}(\bm{x}_{3})\delta\phi_{4}(\bm{x}_{4})}\langle\exp\left(-\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{12x}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x}\right)\rangle\right|_{\phi_{j}=0}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta\phi_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\delta\phi_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})}\langle\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{x}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{x}_{4})\,\exp\left(-\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{12x}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x}\right)\rangle\right|_{\phi_{j}=0}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})}\langle\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{x}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{x}_{4})\left(-\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})-\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{12x}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}\phi_{1}(\bm{x}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)\rangle\right|_{\phi_{j}=0}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{x}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{x}_{4})\rangle-\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{12x}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}\delta(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2})\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{x}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{x}_{4})\rangle.

(178)

The Fourier transform of the second term yields
\lambda_{12x}i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[33,22x3]}
(use (
106
) and (
91
)) as expected. Thus, the 4-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
will be renormalized by the addition of
i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[33,22x3]}
. By design, this is what the counterterm action
\lambda_{12x}\lambda_{34x}S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
contributes.

We argued that the terms
S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}
and
S_{1234}^{\text{asym, ct}\,(4)}
renormalize all 3-point functions as well as the 4-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
. The renormalized correlators are given by (
83
) and (
•
‣
4.2.1
) with the amplitudes on the right hand side replaced by the renormalized amplitudes,

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{i}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{j}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{k}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle_{\text{ren}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{ijx}\,i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j}\Delta_{x}]}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

(179)

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{3}(\bm{k}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{4}(\bm{k}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle_{\text{ren}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{12x}\lambda_{34x}\,i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}+\lambda_{1234}\,i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}.

(180)

As we can see, we obtain the renormalized correlators by simply replacing the amplitudes by the renormalized amplitudes listed in Section
4.3
.

\lambda_{12x}

Figure 4:
All non-vanishing exchange 4-point functions in the asymmetric theory. On the left, we have two 3-vertices present in the action (
4.2
). The diagrams at the top on the right-hand side represent schematically (dropping all symmetry factors) the 4-point functions
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{1}\rangle
,
\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x}\mathcal{O}_{x}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{x}\rangle
, while those at the bottom represent
\langle\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{3}\rangle
,
\langle\mathcal{O}_{4}\mathcal{O}_{x}\mathcal{O}_{x}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{x}\rangle
. Together, these are the six correlators in (
181
). The middle line corresponds to the 4-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
obtained by combining the two vertices.

The asymmetric theory contains additional non-vanishing 4-point functions, namely those in (
•
‣
4.2.1
). To complete our analysis, we should renormalize these as well. This is achieved by
S_{\text{cross}}^{\text{asym, ct}\,(4)}
, the last term in (
173
). To see that this indeed works, first we have to consider the contributions to the 4-point function from the 3-point counterterms in
S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}
. To do this, first notice that we only have three correlators to consider, depending on the dimensions of the operators involved:

\langle\mathcal{O}_{i}^{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{[2]}\rangle,\qquad\langle\mathcal{O}_{i}^{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{[3]}\rangle,\qquad\langle\mathcal{O}_{i}^{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{i}^{[3]}\rangle.

(181)

There are six possible correlators in each case, by choosing
(ij)
from
(12)
,
(1x)
,
(2x)
,
(34)
,
(3x)
,
(4x)
as shown in Figure
4
. The analysis is similar in all cases, however, so we will concentrate on, say,
(ij)=(12)
and the counterterm
\lambda_{12x}S^{\text{ct}\,(3)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}
. Finally, for the counterterm to contribute, it must be non-vanishing when we set
\phi_{x}=0
, since we will not be differentiating with respect to this source. An inspection of (
86
)-(
89
) shows that there are only two cases where the contribution to the 4-point function from the 3-point counterterm action is non-vanishing: (
i
)
\Delta_{1}=3,\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{x}=2
and (
ii
)
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{x}=3
. The relevant form of the counterterm actions,
S^{\text{ct}(3)}_{[322]}
and
S^{\text{ct}(3)}_{[333]}
with
\phi_{x}
set to zero, is identical for both cases up to the overall normalization,

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}(3)}_{I}=c_{I}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{I}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x},

(182)

where
c_{I}=2
for
I=[322]
and
c_{I}=1/3
for
I=[333]
. By taking four functional derivatives, we find

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta^{4}}{\delta\phi_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\delta\phi_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\delta\phi_{2}(\bm{x}_{3})\delta\phi_{1}(\bm{x}_{4})}\langle\exp\left(-c_{I}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{I}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{x}\right)\rangle\right|_{\phi_{j}=0}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{x}_{4})\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-c_{I}\Div_{1}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}_{I}\left[\,\delta(\bm{x}_{3}-\bm{x}_{4})\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{3})\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2})\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{x}_{4})\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{x}_{4})\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{4})\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\delta(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{3})\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{2}(\bm{x}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{1}(\bm{x}_{4})\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+c_{I}^{2}\Div_{1}^{2}(\mu)\mathfrak{a}^{2}_{I}\left[\delta(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2})\delta(\bm{x}_{3}-\bm{x}_{4})\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{3})\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\delta(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{3})\delta(\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{x}_{4})\langle\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{x}(\bm{x}_{2})\rangle\right].

(183)

Fourier transforming this expression, we see that the counterterm contribution from the 3-point counterterm action
S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(3)}
to the 4-point functions
\langle\mathcal{O}_{i}\mathcal{O}_{j}\mathcal{O}_{j}\mathcal{O}_{i}\rangle
reads

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j},\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2};\bm{k}_{3},\bm{k}_{4})+i^{\text{ct}({3})}_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j},\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{3};\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{4}).

(184)

These two terms correspond to the two terms in the regulated correlator itself in (
•
‣
4.2.1
). Thus, what remains to be shown is that the counterterms
S^{\text{asym, ct}\,(4)}_{\text{cross}}
in (
176
) correctly produce

\displaystyle i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j},\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2};\bm{k}_{3},\bm{k}_{4})+i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[\Delta_{i}\Delta_{j},\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{3};\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{4}).

(185)

First, let us consider case (
i
) with
\Delta_{1}=3
and
\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{x}=2
. The counterterm action (
114
) becomes

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[32,23x2]}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{2},\phi_{1})=2\mathfrak{s}_{[32,23x2]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi_{1}^{2}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}.

(186)

By taking four functional derivatives as above, we find that the contribution from this counterterm to the correlation function in momentum space equals twice the expression in (
185
). Thus, we have to introduce the symmetry factor of
1/2
in (
176
). Similarly, for case (
ii
) where
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=\Delta_{x}=3
, the counterterm action (
115
) reads

\displaystyle S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[33,33x3]}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{2},\phi_{1})=2\mathfrak{s}_{[33,33x3]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\left[\phi_{1}^{2}\phi_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}+\phi_{1}\phi_{2}^{2}\mathcal{O}_{1}\right].

(187)

By taking functional derivatives we again find that the counterterm contribution to the 4-point function is twice that of (
185
). Thus, the multiplicative factor of
1/2
in (
176
) is correct in all cases.

Symmetric theory

In this section we consider the most symmetric case: a bulk theory containing only two fields,
\Phi_{[2]}
and
\Phi_{[3]}
, dual to two operators,
\mathcal{O}_{[2]}
of dimension two and
\mathcal{O}_{[3]}
of dimension three. The AdS action contains all possible couplings between the fields,

\displaystyle S^{\text{sym}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{4+2\epsilon}x\sqrt{g}\sum_{\Delta=2,3}\left[\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{[\Delta]}\partial^{\mu}\Phi_{[\Delta]}+\hat{m}^{2}_{\Delta}\Phi_{[\Delta]}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\int\mathrm{d}^{4+2\epsilon}x\sqrt{g}\left[\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[222]}\Phi_{[2]}^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[322]}\Phi_{[3]}\Phi_{[2]}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}\Phi_{[3]}^{2}\Phi_{[2]}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[333]}\Phi_{[3]}^{3}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\int\mathrm{d}^{4+2\epsilon}x\sqrt{g}\left[\frac{1}{24}\lambda_{[2222]}\Phi_{[2]}^{4}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3222]}\Phi_{[3]}\Phi_{[2]}^{3}+\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{[3322]}\Phi_{[3]}^{2}\Phi_{[2]}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3332]}\Phi_{[3]}^{3}\Phi_{[2]}+\frac{1}{24}\lambda_{[3333]}\Phi_{[3]}^{4}\right],

(188)

where we inserted standard symmetry factors for convenience. The coupling constants
\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
and
\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
depend only on the dimensions
\Delta_{j}
of the operators interacting, and not on the order in which they appear. When convenient, we can thus permute the dimensions determining the coupling,
e.g.
,
\lambda_{[322]}=\lambda_{[232]}=\lambda_{[223]}
, and so on for other couplings.

Regulated correlators

Let us derive the regulated correlators in the symmetric theory (
4.5
).

•

With the symmetry factors included in the action, the equations of motion read

\displaystyle(-\Box_{AdS}+\hat{m}^{2}_{2})\Phi_{[2]}=-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[222]}\Phi_{[2]}^{2}-\lambda_{[322]}\Phi_{[3]}\Phi_{[2]}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}\Phi_{[3]}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[2222]}\Phi_{[2]}^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3222]}\Phi_{[3]}\Phi_{[2]}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3322]}\Phi_{[3]}^{2}\Phi_{[2]}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3332]}\Phi_{[3]}^{3},

(189)

\displaystyle(-\Box_{AdS}+\hat{m}^{2}_{3})\Phi_{[3]}=-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[322]}\Phi_{[2]}^{2}-\lambda_{[332]}\Phi_{[3]}\Phi_{[2]}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[333]}\Phi_{[3]}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3222]}\Phi_{[2]}^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3322]}\Phi_{[3]}\Phi_{[2]}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3332]}\Phi_{[2]}\Phi_{[3]}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3333]}\Phi_{[3]}^{3}.

(190)

•

We now solve these equations of motion perturbatively, up to and including terms with three sources. Let us denote by
\Phi_{[\Delta]\{j\}}
the term in the solution which depends on any
(j+1)
sources. This means that
\Phi_{[\Delta]\{0\}}
is the solution to the free field equation. We have

\displaystyle\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}

\displaystyle=\K_{[3]}\ast\phi_{[3]},

(191)

\displaystyle\Phi_{[3]\{1\}}

\displaystyle=-\G_{[3]}\ast\left[\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[333]}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}+\lambda_{[332]}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[322]}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\right],

(192)

\displaystyle\Phi_{[3]\{2\}}

\displaystyle=-\G_{[3]}\ast\left[\lambda_{[333]}\Phi_{[3]\{1\}}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}+\lambda_{[322]}\Phi_{[2]\{1\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+\lambda_{[332]}\left(\Phi_{[3]\{1\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}+\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{1\}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\G_{[3]}\ast\left[\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3333]}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3332]}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3322]}\Phi_{[3]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3222]}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\Phi_{[2]\{0\}}\right],

(193)

where we recall that
\ast
indicates the convolution of the position-space variables (see (
76
)).
Analogous expressions with
2\leftrightarrow 3
follow for
\Phi_{[2]}
. Setting
\psi_{[\Delta]}=\Phi_{[\Delta]\{0\}}=\K_{[\Delta]}\ast\phi_{[\Delta]}
, we can write the expansion of
\Phi_{[3]\{2\}}
in the form

\displaystyle\Phi_{[3]\{2\}}

\displaystyle=\G_{[3]}\ast\left[\left(\lambda_{[333]}\psi_{[3]}+\lambda_{[332]}\psi_{[2]}\right)\G_{[3]}\ast\left(\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[333]}\psi_{[3]}\psi_{[3]}+\lambda_{[332]}\psi_{[3]}\psi_{[2]}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[322]}\psi_{[2]}\psi_{[2]}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\lambda_{[332]}\psi_{[3]}+\lambda_{[322]}\psi_{[2]}\right)\G_{[3]}\ast\left(\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}\psi_{[3]}\psi_{[3]}+\lambda_{[322]}\psi_{[3]}\psi_{[2]}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[222]}\psi_{[2]}\psi_{[2]}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3333]}\psi_{[3]}^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3332]}\psi_{[3]}^{2}\psi_{[2]}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3322]}\psi_{[3]}\psi_{[2]}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3222]}\psi_{[2]}^{3}\right].

(194)

•

All 3-point functions then follow from
\Phi_{[2]\{1\}}
and
\Phi_{[3]\{1\}}
. They have no additional symmetry factors and read

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\bm{k}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle_{\text{reg}}=\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

(195)

for
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}=2,3
.

•

The 4-point functions follow from
\Phi_{[2]\{2\}}
and
\Phi_{[3]\{2\}}
. For example, starting with (
•
‣
4.5.1
), we replace the external propagator
\G_{[3]}
with
-\K_{[3]}
, multiply by
(-1)^{3}
and take three functional derivatives with respect to the sources. All in all, the symmetry factors are selected in such a way that, after all symmetries of the amplitudes are used, every correlator takes the form

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\bm{k}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{4}]}(\bm{k}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle_{\text{reg}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\Delta_{x}=2,3}\left[\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2};\bm{k}_{3},\bm{k}_{4})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{4}]}\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3},\Delta_{2}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{3};\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{4},\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{4};\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{3})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}).

(196)

Note that crossing symmetric terms appear with unit coefficients.

Renormalization

Let us now renormalize the symmetric theory with only two
operators
\mathcal{O}_{[2]}
and
\mathcal{O}_{[3]}
, governed by the bulk action (
4.5
). The regulated 3-point functions are given by (
195
), and 4-point functions by (
196
). As discussed, the renormalized 3- and 4-point functions are found by replacing the regulated amplitudes by the renormalized ones, as listed in Section
4.3
. This gives

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\bm{k}_{3})\rangle\!\rangle_{\text{ren}}=\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}\,i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

(197)

and

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\bm{k}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{[\Delta_{4}]}(\bm{k}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle_{\text{ren}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\Delta_{x}=2,3}\left[\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2};\bm{k}_{3},\bm{k}_{4})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{4}]}i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3},\Delta_{2}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{3};\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[\Delta_{x}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{4},\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}x\Delta_{x}]}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{4};\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{3})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}\,i^{\text{ren}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),

(198)

thus providing explicit expressions for all 3- and 4-point functions in the symmetric theory.

Just as we did for the asymmetric theory in Section
4.4
, let us now specify the counterterm action that renormalizes the theory and analyze its properties. Since we have only two fields that are distinguishable by their dimensions, we may drop all indices in the counterterm actions and define

\displaystyle S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(3)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}

\displaystyle=S^{\text{ct}\,(3)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(\phi_{[d-\Delta_{1}]},\phi_{[d-\Delta_{2}]},\phi_{[d-\Delta_{3}]}),

(199)

\displaystyle S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=S^{\text{ct}\,(4)}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}(\phi_{[d-\Delta_{1}]},\phi_{[d-\Delta_{2}]},\phi_{[d-\Delta_{3}]},\phi_{[d-\Delta_{4}]}).

(200)

Note that some symmetry factors may appear, for example,

\displaystyle S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(3)}_{[322]}=2\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\,\phi_{[0]}\phi_{[1]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}.

(201)

The complete counterterm action is given by

\displaystyle S^{\text{sym, ct}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[222]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(3)}_{[222]}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[322]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(3)}_{[322]}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(3)}_{[332]}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[333]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(3)}_{[333]}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3222]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[3222]}+\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{[3322]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[3322]}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3332]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[3332]}+\frac{1}{24}\lambda_{[3333]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[3333]}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\Delta_{x}=2,3}\left[\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[32\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[22\Delta_{x}]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4}\left(\lambda_{[33\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[22\Delta_{x}]}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}+2\lambda_{[32\Delta_{x}]}^{2}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[33\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[32\Delta_{x}]}\left(S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[32,33x\Delta_{x}]}+S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[33,23x\Delta_{x}]}+S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[33,32x\Delta_{x}]}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{8}\lambda_{[33\Delta_{x}]}^{2}S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[33,33x\Delta_{x}]}\right].

(202)

The symmetry factors here are quite straightforward for the 3-point counterterm actions, but more complicated for the 4-point amplitudes. Since each 3-point function in (
195
) is given by a single amplitude, the corresponding symmetry factors are equal to one over the symmetry factor of the Witten diagram. The same argument holds for contact 4-point functions, which leads to the values listed.

For the exchange 4-point counterterms, we have to remember that the expressions in (
198
) always contain three crossing-symmetric terms.
Consider, for example, the contribution to the 4-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\rangle
. We need the counterterm contribution to each of the three crossing symmetric terms in (
198
). Notice, however, that
i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}
in (
117
) is a constant and thus is trivially crossing symmetric. Hence, we need the contribution of
3i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}
from the action
S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}
. On the other hand,
S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}=\mathfrak{s}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}\int\phi_{[0]}\phi_{[1]}^{3}
and thus after taking the functional derivatives, its contribution to the 4-point function equals
6i^{\text{ct}({4})}_{[3222]}
. This leads to the factor of
1/2
in (
4.5.2
) multiplying
S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[32,22x\Delta_{x}]}
.
Similarly, let us look at the counterterm contribution rendering the 4-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\rangle
finite. The three terms in (
198
) become

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{[33\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[22\Delta_{x}]}\mathfrak{s}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}+2\lambda_{[32\Delta_{x}]}^{2}\mathfrak{s}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}\right)\times\left[\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{3})+\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{4})\right].

(203)

On the other hand, the contributions to the 4-point function from the counterterm actions
S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}
and
S^{\text{sym, ct}\,(4)}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}
are
4\mathfrak{s}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}(\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{3})+\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{4}))
and
4\mathfrak{s}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}(\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{3})+\hat{i}_{[22]}(k_{4}))
respectively. This leads to the factor of
1/4
in (
4.5.2
).

Beta functions

From a QFT point of view, it is more convenient to organize the counterterm action as

\displaystyle S^{\text{sym}}_{\text{ct}}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3+2\epsilon}\bm{x}\left[Z_{[0]}(\phi_{[0]}\mu^{-\epsilon})\phi_{[0]}\mathcal{O}_{[3]}+Z_{[1]}(\phi_{[0]}\mu^{-\epsilon})\phi_{[1]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\mu^{2\epsilon}Z_{[3]}(\phi_{[0]}\mu^{-\epsilon},\phi_{[1]}\mu^{-\epsilon})\bm{1}\ \right].

(204)

In the language of textbook QFT,

Z_{[0]}
and
Z_{[1]}
are multiplicative renormalization factors inducing beta functions for the sources
\phi_{[0]}
and
\phi_{[1]}
respectively, while
Z_{[3]}
is the anomaly-inducing additive renormalization factor. This means that we identify
\phi_{[0]}
and
\phi_{[1]}
as
renormalized sources
, which depend implicitly on the renormalization scale
\mu
. The
bare sources
are the combinations that couple directly to the operators in (
4.5.3
),
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\text{bare}}_{[0]}

\displaystyle=Z_{[0]}(\phi_{[0]}\mu^{-\epsilon})\phi_{[0]},

\displaystyle\phi^{\text{bare}}_{[1]}

\displaystyle=Z_{[1]}(\phi_{[0]}\mu^{-\epsilon})\phi_{[1]}.

(205)

By re-organizing the action (
4.5.2
), we find the multiplicative renormalization factors

\displaystyle Z_{[0]}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[333]}\mathfrak{s}_{[333]}\phi_{[0]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left[\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3333]}\mathfrak{s}_{[3333]}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\Delta_{x}=2,3}\lambda_{[33\Delta_{x}]}^{2}\mathfrak{s}_{[33,33x\Delta_{x}]}\right]\phi_{[0]}^{2}+O(\phi_{[0]}^{3}),

(206)

\displaystyle Z_{[1]}

\displaystyle=1+\lambda_{[322]}\mathfrak{s}_{[322]}\phi_{[0]}+\left[\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3322]}\mathfrak{s}_{[3322]}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\Delta_{x}=2,3}\left(2\lambda_{[32\Delta_{x}]}^{2}\mathfrak{s}_{[32,32x\Delta_{x}]}+\lambda_{[33\Delta_{x}]}\lambda_{[22\Delta_{x}]}\mathfrak{s}_{[33,22x\Delta_{x}]}\right)\right]\phi_{[0]}^{2}+O(\phi_{[0]}^{3}).

(207)

It is now straightforward to compute the beta functions for the two couplings,

\displaystyle\beta_{\phi_{[n]}}=\mu\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\phi_{[n]}.

(208)

To do this, we invert the relations (
205
) in order to express the renormalized sources in terms of the bare, scale-independent sources. In this way, we find

\displaystyle\beta_{\phi_{[0]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[333]}\phi_{[0]}^{2}+\left[-\frac{1}{18}\lambda_{[3333]}+\frac{1}{12}\lambda_{[332]}^{2}-\frac{1}{54}\lambda_{[333]}^{2}\right]\phi_{[0]}^{3}+O(\phi_{[0]}^{4}),

(209)

\displaystyle\beta_{\phi_{[1]}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{[322]}\phi_{[0]}\phi_{[1]}+\left[-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3322]}-\lambda_{[322]}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{[222]}\lambda_{[332]}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{1}{18}(1+3\mathfrak{a}^{(1)}_{[322]}-3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})\lambda_{[322]}\lambda_{[333]}\right]\phi_{[0]}^{2}\phi_{[1]}+O(\phi_{[0]}^{3}).

(210)

Notice that the second term in
\beta_{\phi_{[1]}}
is scheme-dependent. However, this is not in contradiction with the well-known statement that the first two terms in the beta function are scheme-independent.
Rather, this happens because the beta functions are calculated for the dimensionful regulated sources
\phi_{[0]}
and
\phi_{[1]}
. If instead we were to look at the dimensionless couplings
g_{[0]}
and
g_{[1]}
, defined as

\displaystyle g_{[0]}=\phi_{[0]}\mu^{-\epsilon},\qquad g_{[1]}=\phi_{[1]}\mu^{-1-\epsilon},

(211)

we would obtain beta functions

\displaystyle\beta_{g_{[0]}}

\displaystyle=-\epsilon g_{[0]}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[333]}g_{[0]}^{2}+\left[-\frac{1}{18}\lambda_{[3333]}+\frac{1}{12}\lambda_{[332]}^{2}-\frac{1}{54}\lambda_{[333]}^{2}\right]g_{[0]}^{3}+O(g_{[0]}^{4}),

(212)

\displaystyle\beta_{g_{[1]}}

\displaystyle=-(1+\epsilon)g_{[1]}+\lambda_{[322]}g_{[0]}g_{[1]}+\left[-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3322]}-\lambda_{[322]}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{[222]}\lambda_{[332]}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{1}{18}(1+3\mathfrak{a}^{(1)}_{[322]}-3\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})\lambda_{[322]}\lambda_{[333]}\right]g_{[0]}^{2}g_{[1]}+O(g_{[0]}^{3}),

(213)

where, as customary, we kept the terms of order
\epsilon
in the classical scaling term. The beta function for
g_{[1]}
now contains a non-vanishing linear term so that the first two terms are indeed scheme-independent.

Anomalies

The dependence of the correlation functions on the renormalization scale
\mu
is governed by the Callan-Symanzik equation. Let
W[\phi_{[0]},\phi_{[1]};\mu]
be the renormalized generating functional and
\phi_{[0]}
and
\phi_{[1]}
denote the renormalized sources. The chain rule then leads to the Callan-Symanzik equation,

\displaystyle\mu\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}W=\left[\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}+\sum_{j=0,1}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\,\beta_{\phi_{[j]}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi_{[j]}(\bm{x})}\right]W=\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\,\mathcal{A},

(214)

where
\mathcal{A}
is the anomaly. The anomaly reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{(3)}+\mathcal{A}^{(4)},

(215)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[222]}\phi_{[1]}^{3}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}\phi_{[1]}\partial_{\mu}\phi_{[0]}\partial^{\mu}\phi_{[0]},

(216)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\phi_{[0]}\phi_{[1]}^{3}\,\left[\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[322]}\lambda_{[222]}(1+\mathfrak{a}_{[222]}^{(1)}-\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)})+\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{[332]}\lambda_{[322]}+\frac{1}{6}\lambda_{[3222]}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{1}{6}\phi_{[0]}^{3}\partial^{2}\phi_{[1]}-\frac{1}{2}\phi_{[0]}^{2}\phi_{[1]}\partial^{2}\phi_{[0]}\right)\times\left[-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[3332]}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{[332]}\lambda_{[322]}(2+\mathfrak{a}_{[332]}^{(1)}-\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)})-\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{[333]}\lambda_{[332]}(\tfrac{25}{12}+\mathfrak{a}_{[332]}^{(1)}-\mathfrak{a}_{[333]}^{(1)})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\phi_{[0]}^{3}\partial^{2}\phi_{[1]}\,\left[\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{[332]}\lambda_{[322]}+\frac{1}{9}\lambda_{[333]}\lambda_{[332]}\right].

(217)

As we can see the anomaly is scheme-dependent, which means that some terms can be eliminated by the suitable choice of the counterterm constants.

The Callan-Symanzik equation (
214
) serves as a check on our results. First, we act with suitable functional derivatives on both sides in order to translate it into a statement on the correlators. As an example, consider the renormalized 4-point function
\langle\mathcal{O}_{[3]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\mathcal{O}_{[2]}\rangle
. The explicit derivative of (
196
) with respect to the scale
\mu
reads

\displaystyle\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{[3]}(\bm{k}_{1})\mathcal{O}_{[2]}(\bm{k}_{2})\mathcal{O}_{[2]}(\bm{k}_{3})\mathcal{O}_{[2]}(\bm{k}_{4})\rangle\!\rangle=\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{[332]}\lambda_{[322]}+\lambda_{[3222]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{[322]}\lambda_{[222]}\left[-\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}l_{24+}l_{23+}}{\mu^{3}}\right)+3-3\mathfrak{a}_{[322]}^{(1)}\right].

(218)

It is easy to see that the only relevant term in the beta functions is
\lambda_{[322]}\phi_{[0]}\phi_{[1]}
present in
\beta_{[1]}
. The corresponding term in the Callan-Symanzik equation, Fourier-transformed into momentum space, reads

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta^{4}}{\delta\phi_{[0]}(\bm{x}_{1})\delta\phi_{[1]}(\bm{x}_{2})\delta\phi_{[1]}(\bm{x}_{3})\delta\phi_{[1]}(\bm{x}_{4})}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\bm{x}\,\lambda_{[322]}\phi_{[0]}\phi_{[1]}\frac{\delta W}{\delta\phi_{[1]}}\right|_{\text{Fourier}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\lambda_{[322]}\lambda_{[222]}\left[\log\left(\frac{l_{34+}l_{24+}l_{23+}}{\mu^{3}}\right)+3\mathfrak{a}_{[222]}^{(1)}\right].

(219)

When the two terms are added together, the logarithmic terms cancel, leading to an ultralocal expression. This is an important check on our results as the anomaly in a local theory must be ultralocal. It is now straightforward to check that the remaining sum is obtained by acting with the functional derivatives on the anomaly
\mathcal{A}^{(3)}
in (
216
).

Change of scheme

The aim of this section is to present a comprehensive analysis allowing for a change of the regularization scheme. The amplitudes presented in Section
3
are regulated in the half-integer scheme (
1
). In this section, we show how to obtain the amplitudes regulated in the general scheme (
2
),
i.e.
,

\displaystyle d\longmapsto\hat{d}=d+2u\epsilon,

\displaystyle\Delta_{j}\longmapsto\hat{\Delta}_{j}=\Delta_{j}+(u+v_{j})\epsilon,

(220)

where
u
and
v_{j}
for
j=1,2,3,4,x
are arbitrary regularization parameters. There are several motivations for this:

•

The half-integer scheme we used so far is very convenient, but different regularization schemes may be needed for other
computations. In general, one needs to shift all parameters by different amounts, as in (
220
), in order to regulate all correlators.
For example, in the computation of tensorial correlators involving conserved currents and/or stress tensors, the general scheme is typically required as discussed in
Bzowski:2015pba
;
Bzowski:2017poo
;
Bzowski:2018fql
.

•

Another motivation arises from applications to inflationary cosmology.
As discussed in
Bzowski:2012ih
;
McFadden:2013ria
, a certain class of slow-roll models can be described holographically in terms of a three-dimensional CFT deformed by a marginally relevant scalar of dimension
\Delta=3-\lambda
. The small parameter
\lambda\ll 1
is related to the slow-roll parameters and the spectral tilt, and allows one to compute cosmological correlators using conformal perturbation theory. For such purposes, we want to know amplitudes in the regularization scheme
u=0
and
v_{j}=-1
,
i.e.
, for
d=3
and
\Delta=3-\epsilon
, where the regulator
\epsilon
now plays the role of
\lambda
.

In the following, since our focus will now be on the
u
and
v_{j}
dependence of the regulated amplitudes, we will drop all other variables and denote
\hat{i}=\hat{i}(u,v_{j})
.

2-point amplitudes

In the general regularization scheme (
220
), 2-point amplitudes follow from (
66
) and the expansion of the bulk-to-boundary propagator (
3
). With
\hat{d}=3+2u\epsilon
and
\hat{\Delta}=\Delta+(u+v)\epsilon
, one finds

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[22]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-v\epsilon\right)}{4^{v\epsilon}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+v\epsilon\right)}k^{1+2v\epsilon},\qquad\quad\hat{i}_{[33]}=-\frac{\Gamma\left(-\frac{1}{2}-v\epsilon\right)}{4^{1+v\epsilon}\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}+v\epsilon\right)}k^{3+2v\epsilon}.

(221)

3-point amplitudes

In this subsection we address the scheme-change procedure for 3-point amplitudes, following the discussion in
Bzowski:2015pba
and
Bzowski:2015yxv
. In general, it is difficult to calculate directly the amplitudes in the general regularization scheme (
220
) with arbitrary
u,v_{j}
. However, if we know the regulated amplitude for some specific choice
\bar{u},\bar{v}_{j}
,
\hat{i}(\bar{u},\bar{v}_{j})
, then it is possible to compute the additional terms needed to obtain
the regulated amplitude
\hat{i}(u,v_{j})
for general
u,v_{j}
. As we already know the results in the half-integer scheme with
\bar{u}=1
and
\bar{v}_{j}=0
from Section
3
, this will be our starting point.

As discussed in
Bzowski:2015yxv
, the scheme-dependent terms are contained in a specific part of the regulated amplitude, which also contains all divergent terms. The idea is then to isolate this part, which we will call the
parameter-dependent
or
scheme-dependent
part. The remaining part is finite and scheme-independent, and can be obtained from the half-integer scheme.
We computed in full generality the finite scheme-dependent part of 3-point functions in
Bzowski:2015yxv
, but for our purposes here we also need the order
\epsilon
scheme-dependent part. The reason is that this part will be needed in the computation of finite scheme-dependent terms in 4-point exchange diagrams.

We begin this section by reviewing the procedure for scheme change discussed in
Bzowski:2015yxv
, and showing how the procedure can be simplified when the starting point is the half-integer scheme (
1
). Our main goal is to derive the 3-point amplitudes, including parameter-dependent terms up to and including terms of order
\epsilon
. This will be essential for the derivation of the exchange 4-point functions in the general regularization scheme (
220
) in Section
5.4
. Furthermore, for this same reason, we also must calculate 3-point-like amplitudes with one Bessel-
K
function in (
7
) replaced by a Bessel-
I
.

Definitions

Let
\mathcal{I}
denote the integrand in (
7
). Its power expansion around
z=0
reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\>\sum_{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}=0}^{\infty}b_{n_{1}}(\sigma_{1}\hat{\beta}_{1})b_{n_{2}}(\sigma_{2}\hat{\beta}_{2})b_{n_{3}}(\sigma_{3}\hat{\beta}_{3})z^{\frac{\hat{d}}{2}-1+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\sigma_{j}\hat{\beta}_{j}+2n_{j})},

(222)

where the coefficients
b_{n}
are defined in (
501
) and the regulated
\beta_{j}
parameters are given as usual by

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{j}=\hat{\Delta}_{j}-\frac{\hat{d}}{2}=\beta_{j}+v_{j}\epsilon,

\displaystyle\beta_{j}=\Delta_{j}-\frac{d}{2}.

(223)

Let us rearrange the sum as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}z^{L(\sigma_{j},n)-1},

(224)

where the non-vanishing coefficients and the corresponding
L
s are parametrized by three signs,
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1
and a non-negative integer
n
,

\displaystyle L(\sigma_{j},n)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{d}}{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sigma_{j}\hat{\beta}_{j}+2n,

(225)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geq 0\\
n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n\end{subarray}}b_{n_{1}}(\sigma_{1}\hat{\beta}_{1})b_{n_{2}}(\sigma_{2}\hat{\beta}_{2})b_{n_{3}}(\sigma_{3}\hat{\beta}_{3}).

(226)

Next we analyze the
\epsilon
-dependence. From (
225
), we see that
L
is linear in the regulator,

\displaystyle L(\sigma_{j},n)=L_{0}(\sigma_{j},n)+\epsilon\,L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}(u,v_{j}),

(227)

where

\displaystyle L_{0}(\sigma_{j},n)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sigma_{j}\beta_{j}+2n,

\displaystyle L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}(u,v_{j})=u+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sigma_{j}v_{j}\,,

(228)

and all scheme-dependence in
L
enters via
L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}(u,v_{j})
.

The coefficients
\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}
depend on the regulator
\epsilon
and
v_{j}
but not on
u
. This follows from the fact that all dependence on the regulators enters through
\hat{\beta}_{j}
, and
\hat{\beta}_{j}
depends only on
\epsilon
and
v_{j}
. Actually,
in the cases we analyze in this paper (
\beta_{j}
non-integral and positive) the coefficients are smooth as
\epsilon\to 0
. It follows that

\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}=\mathcal{I}^{0}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}+\epsilon\sum_{k=1}^{3}\mathcal{I}^{1}_{(\sigma_{j},n)k}v_{k}+O(\epsilon^{2}),

(229)

where both
\mathcal{I}^{0}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}
and
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{(\sigma_{j},n)k}
are independent of
u
and
v_{j}
.

As shown in
Bzowski:2015yxv
, all divergences and finite
u
and
v_{j}
-dependent terms for 3-point amplitudes (
7
) are contained in
i^{\text{div}}
defined by

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}(u,v_{j};\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{L_{0}=0\}}\int_{0}^{\mu^{-1}}\mathrm{d}z\,\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}z^{-1+\epsilon L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}}=\sum_{\{L_{0}=0\}}\frac{\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}\mu^{-\epsilon L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}}}{\epsilon L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}},

(230)

where the sum is over all signs
\sigma_{j}
and
n
such that
L_{0}=0
and
\mu>0
is an arbitrary number.
Using (
228
), the condition
L_{0}=0
may be solved to determine
n
in terms of the signs
\sigma_{j}
,
n=n(\sigma_{j})
,
and we define

\mathcal{I}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n(\sigma_{j}))}&\qquad n(\sigma_{j})=0,1,2,\ldots,\\
0&\qquad{\rm otherwise}\,.\end{array}\right.

(231)

Then

i^{\text{div}}(u,v_{j};\mu)=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}\mu^{-\epsilon L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}}}{\epsilon L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}},

(232)

where the sum is now over all signs
\sigma_{j}
. Due to the fact that all amplitudes discussed in this paper are at most linearly divergent and renormalizable by counterterms, there are only two possibilities to yield a non-zero
\mathcal{I}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}
. Either
\mathcal{I}_{---}\neq 0
with all other choices of signs vanishing, or some of
\mathcal{I}_{+--},\mathcal{I}_{-+-},\mathcal{I}_{--+}
are non-zero (possibly all three), while all other combinations vanish. This can be seen from Table
2
: as discussed at length in
Bzowski:2015pba
, anomalies are associated with
(---)
singularities and beta functions with
(--+)
ones.

In
Bzowski:2015yxv
we have shown the following procedure for scheme change:

Fact 1

If we already know the amplitude
\hat{i}(\bar{u},\bar{v}_{j})
regulated in
some
scheme with parameters
\bar{u}
and
\bar{v}_{j}
, we can use (
232
) to obtain the amplitude in
any
regularization scheme,

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})=\hat{i}(\bar{u},\bar{v}_{j})+\left[i^{\text{div}}(u,v_{j})-i^{\text{div}}(\bar{u},\bar{v}_{j})\right]+O(\epsilon).

(233)

From now on, we will concentrate on the amplitudes we are interested in for this paper,
i.e.
,
d=3
and all conformal dimensions
\Delta_{j}
equal to
2
or
3
. For these
d
and
\Delta_{j}
the 3-point functions are at most linearly divergent. The divergence thus can only emerge from the explicit divergence in (
232
). In particular, the coefficients
\mathcal{I}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}}
are finite and continuous in the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit.

Scheme change

Equation (
233
) can be greatly simplified and generalized when we start from the half-integer scheme with
\bar{v}_{j}=0
. First note that, given an amplitude regulated in the half-integer scheme with
u=1
and
v_{j}=0
, we can obtain for free the amplitude regulated with general
u
simply by rescaling
\epsilon\mapsto u\epsilon
. Thus, knowing
\hat{i}(1,0)
to all orders in
\epsilon
, we also know
\hat{i}(u,0)
to all orders in
\epsilon
.

Let us also define
\hat{i}|_{\epsilon^{n}}
to represent the terms of order
\epsilon^{n}
in a regulated amplitude
\hat{i}
, so that up to linear order a linearly divergent amplitude is given by

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})=\frac{\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{-1}}}{\epsilon}+\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{0}}+\epsilon\,\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{1}}+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(234)

With this notation in place, one can show the following:

Fact 2

Consider a linearly divergent amplitude
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
regulated in the general
(u,v_{j})
-scheme. The expansion coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{-1}}

\displaystyle=i^{\text{div}}\left(u,v_{j};1\right)|_{\epsilon^{-1}},

(235)

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{0}}

\displaystyle=\hat{i}(1,0)|_{\epsilon^{0}}+i^{\text{div}}\left(u,v_{j};1\right)|_{\epsilon^{0}},

(236)

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{1}}

\displaystyle=\hat{i}(u,0)|_{\epsilon^{1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\epsilon v_{j}\frac{\partial\hat{i}}{\partial\beta_{j}}(u,0)|_{\epsilon^{1}}+i^{\text{div}}\left(u,v_{j};1\right)|_{\epsilon^{1}}.

(237)

Not only can we evaluate the finite part for general
(u,v_{j})
, we can also evaluate the subleading terms of order
\epsilon
provided we know the derivatives of the regulated amplitude with respect to the Bessel indices
\beta_{j}
. At half-integral Bessel indices the derivatives with respect to the order are known explicitly, see equations (
502
) – (
505
). Furthermore, we will need the expansion of the amplitude to order
\epsilon^{1}
when we discuss the scheme change for exchange 4-point functions.

Example

To illustrate our discussion consider the amplitude
\hat{i}_{[222]}
regulated in a scheme with general
u
and
v_{[2]}
for all dimensions,
\hat{\Delta}_{j}=2+(u+v_{[2]})\epsilon
for
j=1,2,3
. First, let us recall the expression we have already computed in the

(1,0)
scheme,

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[222]}(1,0)

\displaystyle=\Div_{1}(k_{t})=\Gamma(\epsilon)k_{t}^{-\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon}-(\log k_{t}+\gamma_{E})+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\left((\log k_{t}+\gamma_{E})^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\right)+O(\epsilon^{2}),

(238)

where
k_{t}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
.

To work out the change of scheme we need
i^{\text{div}}_{[222]}
. In this case, there is only one choice of signs for which

L_{0}=0
yields a non-negative integer
n
, namely all minus, which yields
n=0
and
L_{---}=u-3v_{[2]}
so that

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[222]}(u,v_{[2]};\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu^{-1}}\mathrm{d}z\,z^{-1+\epsilon(u-3v_{[2]})}=\frac{\mu^{-\epsilon(u-3v_{[2]})}}{\epsilon\,(u-3v_{[2]})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon\,(u-3v_{[2]})}+\log\mu+\frac{1}{2}(u-3v_{[2]})\log^{2}\mu\,\epsilon+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(239)

To use Fact
2
, we evaluate at
\mu=1
to obtain

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[222]}(u,v_{[2]};1)|_{\epsilon^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon\,(u-3v_{[2]})},\qquad i^{\text{div}}_{[222]}(u,v_{[2]};1)|_{\epsilon^{n}}=0,\ n\geq 0.

(240)

We also need
\hat{i}_{[222]}(u,0)
which may be obtained from
(
5.2.3
) by
\epsilon\mapsto u\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[222]}(u,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{u\epsilon}-\left(\log k_{t}+\gamma_{E}\right)+\frac{u\epsilon}{2}\left[(\log k_{t}+\gamma_{E})^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\right]+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(241)

The final and the most difficult step is to calculate the terms involving the derivatives with respect to the Bessel order. Using (
502
) and (
508
) we find

\displaystyle\left.\epsilon v_{[2]}\frac{\partial\hat{i}_{[222]}}{\partial\beta_{1}}\right|_{\beta_{1}=\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\epsilon v_{[2]}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}z\,e^{-zk_{t}}z^{-1+u\epsilon}\left(e^{2zk_{1}}E_{1}(2zk_{1})+\log k_{1}+\log 2+\gamma_{E}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{v_{[2]}}{u^{2}\epsilon}+\left[-\Li_{2}\left(\frac{-k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}}{k_{t}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\log k_{t}+\gamma_{E}\right)^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\right]v_{[2]}\epsilon+O(\epsilon^{2}).

(242)

We can put everything together to find the amplitude
\hat{i}_{[222]}
to linear order in
\epsilon
and in an arbitrary regularization scheme. According to Fact
2
,

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[222]}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{u-3v_{[2]}},

(243)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[222]}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{0}}

\displaystyle=-\log k_{t}-\gamma_{E},

(244)

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[222]}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{u+3v_{[2]}}{2}\left[(\log k_{t}+\gamma_{E})^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-v_{[2]}\left[\Li_{2}\left(\frac{l_{12-}}{k_{t}}\right)+\Li_{2}\left(\frac{l_{13-}}{k_{t}}\right)+\Li_{2}\left(\frac{l_{23-}}{k_{t}}\right)\right],

(245)

where
l_{12-}=k_{1}+k_{2}-|\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2}|=k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}
and similarly for
l_{13-}
and
l_{23-}
.

This example is slightly degenerate as it does not contain any parameter-dependent finite piece. To illustrate this point consider the case
\hat{i}_{[332]}
, and to simplify the discussion, we consider the two operators of dimension three as identical so that
\hat{\Delta}_{j}=3+(u+v_{[3]})\epsilon
for
j=1,2
and
\hat{\Delta}_{2}=2+(u+v_{[2]})\epsilon
. The
L_{0}=0
condition is satisfied with all signs negative and
n=1

so that
L_{---}=u-2v_{[3]}-v_{[2]}
. Following the same steps, we find

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}_{[332]}(u,v_{[2]},v_{[3]};\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mu^{-1}}\mathrm{d}z\,z^{-1+\epsilon\,L_{---}}\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{k_{3}^{2}}{1-2v_{[2]}\epsilon}-\frac{k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}}{1+2v_{[3]}\epsilon}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{3}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}}{2\epsilon\,L_{---}}+\frac{(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})v_{[3]}+k_{3}^{2}v_{[2]}}{L_{---}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}(k_{3}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})\log\mu+O(\epsilon).

(246)

The first term is an unambiguous divergence, which for
u=1
and
v_{[j]}=0
matches
\hat{i}_{[322]}
. The second term is a finite, scheme-dependent piece and depends only on
v_{j}
but not
u
. The last term contains the cut-off scale, but otherwise is scheme-independent. This is the general structure, which holds in the generic case. Using Fact
2
, we find

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[332]}(u,v_{j})=\frac{k_{3}^{2}-k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2}}{2(u-v_{[2]}-2v_{[3]})\,\epsilon}+\hat{i}_{[332]}(1,0)|_{\epsilon^{0}}+\frac{(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})v_{[3]}+k_{3}^{2}v_{[2]}}{u-v_{[2]}-2v_{[3]}}+O(\epsilon).

(247)

The finite part here requires the expansion of the regulated amplitude (
22
) to order
\epsilon^{0}
, yielding

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[332]}(1,0)|_{\epsilon^{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-k_{3}^{2})\left(\log k_{t}+\gamma_{E}\right)-\frac{1}{4}(k_{1}+k_{2}-3k_{3})(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}).

(248)

This provides the expression for
\hat{i}_{[332]}
regulated in an arbitrary scheme through order
\epsilon^{0}
. The
\epsilon^{1}
terms may be computed as in the previous example. The final result through order
\epsilon^{1}
is given in (
313
)-(
318
).

4-point contact amplitudes

The scheme change procedure for 4-point (and in fact, any
n
-point) contact amplitudes is completely analogous to that of 3-point amplitudes. We define the analogue of (
228
) with four signs,

\displaystyle L_{0}(\sigma_{j},n)

\displaystyle=d+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\sigma_{j}\beta_{j}+2n,\qquad L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}}(u,v_{j})=u+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\sigma_{j}v_{j}

(249)

and then (
232
) becomes

\displaystyle i^{\text{div}}(u,v_{j};\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}=\pm 1}\frac{\mathcal{I}_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}}}(v_{j})\mu^{-\epsilon L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}}}}{\epsilon L_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}}},

(250)

where
\mathcal{I}_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}}}(v_{j})
is defined by the analogous equation to (
231
).
As in the case of 3-point functions, in all the cases we discuss in this paper only one combination signs gives a non-zero
\mathcal{I}_{{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}}}
, and in all cases these signs are either all minus (corresponding to anomalies), or one plus and three minus (corresponding to beta functions).
The analogue of Fact
2
then holds:

Fact 3

Consider a linearly divergent amplitude
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}
regulated in an arbitrary
u,v_{j}
-scheme. The expansion coefficients can be extracted as follows,

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{-1}}

\displaystyle=i^{\text{div}}\left(u,v_{j};1\right)|_{\epsilon^{-1}},

(251)

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{0}}

\displaystyle=\hat{i}(1,0)|_{\epsilon^{0}}+i^{\text{div}}\left(u,v_{j};1\right)|_{\epsilon^{0}},

(252)

\displaystyle\hat{i}(u,v_{j})|_{\epsilon^{1}}

\displaystyle=\hat{i}(u,0)|_{\epsilon^{1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\epsilon v_{j}\frac{\partial\hat{i}}{\partial\beta_{j}}(u,0)|_{\epsilon^{1}}+i^{\text{div}}\left(u,v_{j};1\right)|_{\epsilon^{1}}.

(253)

The generalization to
n
-point contact amplitudes is straightforward.

4-point exchange diagrams

In this subsection, we present a procedure that allows one to evaluate the 4-point exchange amplitude in the general regularization scheme (
2
). The idea is the same as in our discussion of 3-point functions: we would like to split the regulated exchange amplitude
\hat{i}
into two parts, with one of them,
i^{\text{div}}
, containing all scheme-dependent terms, and then apply the analogue of Fact
1
. We will present such a split, but our construction is not optimal. There is a natural ambiguity in that one may always add to a given
i^{\text{div}}
additional scheme-independent terms, and such terms would not contribute to
\hat{i}(u,v_{j})
. Ideally,
i^{\text{div}}
should not contain any scheme-independent terms, but our procedure will produce
i^{\text{div}}
containing (a large number of) such scheme-independent terms.

Definitions

We begin with a number of definitions. Since the bulk-to-bulk propagator (
•
‣
2
) contains a Bessel-
I
function, we will need 3-point-like amplitudes with one Bessel-
K
replaced by a Bessel-
I
. To do this, we first define

\displaystyle\II_{d,\Delta}(z,k)=2^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}-1}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\right)k^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}z^{\frac{d}{2}}I_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(kz)

(254)

so that we can rewrite the bulk-to-bulk propagator as

\displaystyle\G_{d,\Delta}(z,k;\zeta)=k^{-(2\Delta-d)}\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\II_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(kz)\K_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(k\zeta)&\text{ for }z<\zeta,\\
\K_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(kz)\II_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(k\zeta)&\text{ for }z>\zeta.\end{array}\right.

Next, we define the 3-point amplitude (
7
) with
\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{3}]}
replaced by
\hat{\II}_{[\Delta_{3}]}
(
i.e.
, the regulated amplitude
\II_{d,\Delta}
) as

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}z\,z^{-\hat{d}-1}\,\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(z,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(z,k_{2})\hat{\II}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(z,k_{3}).

(257)

The properties of these amplitudes are very similar to those of the actual 3-point amplitudes (
7
). In particular
j^{\text{div}}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}
is defined analogously to (
232
) and Fact
2
holds as well.

Furthermore, we generalize the amplitudes
\hat{i}
and
\hat{j}
by leaving the upper limits of integration open,

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(z;k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}\mathrm{d}\zeta\,\zeta^{-\hat{d}-1}\,\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\zeta,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\zeta,k_{2})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\zeta,k_{3}),

(258)

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}(z;k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}\mathrm{d}\zeta\,\zeta^{-\hat{d}-1}\,\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\zeta,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\zeta,k_{2})\hat{\II}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\zeta,k_{3}),

(259)

so that
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}=\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}(\infty)
and
\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}=\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}(\infty)
. Finally, we promote the explicit power of the integration variable to another parameter,

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{\alpha[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\zeta\,\zeta^{\alpha}\,\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\zeta,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\zeta,k_{2})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\zeta,k_{3}),

(260)

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{\alpha[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\zeta\,\zeta^{\alpha}\,\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(\zeta,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(\zeta,k_{2})\hat{\II}_{[\Delta_{3}]}(\zeta,k_{3}),

(261)

so that
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}=\hat{i}_{-\hat{d}-1,[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}]}
and
\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}=\hat{j}_{-\hat{d}-1,[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}]}
.

Change of scheme

Using the bulk-to-bulk propagator (
5.4.1
) and

\int_{z}^{\infty}=\int_{0}^{\infty}-\int_{0}^{z}

we can rewrite the 4-point exchange amplitude (
•
‣
2
) as

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=s^{-2\hat{\beta}_{x}}\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{x}]}(k_{1},k_{2},s)\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}(k_{3},k_{4},s)

\displaystyle\qquad-s^{-2\hat{\beta}_{x}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}z\,z^{-\hat{d}-1}\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(z,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(z,k_{2})\hat{\II}_{[\Delta_{x}]}(z,s)\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}(z;k_{3},k_{4},s)

\displaystyle\qquad+s^{-2\hat{\beta}_{x}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}z\,z^{-\hat{d}-1}\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{1}]}(z,k_{1})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{2}]}(z,k_{2})\hat{\K}_{[\Delta_{x}]}(z,s)\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4};\Delta_{x}]}(z;k_{3},k_{4},s).

(262)

The
z
-dependent amplitudes
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}(z;k_{3},k_{4},s)
and
\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4};\Delta_{x}]}(z;k_{3},k_{4},s)
can now be computed after
power expanding the integrands using (
498
) and (
500
). Analogously to (
224
), let
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
denote the integrands in (
258
) and (
259
) respectively. Furthermore, let
\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}
denote the coefficient of
\zeta^{L-1}
in the power expansion of
\mathcal{I}
, and
\mathcal{J}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)}
the coefficient of
\zeta^{L^{\prime}-1}
in the power expansion of
\mathcal{J}
. Then

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}(z;k_{3},k_{4},s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}(k_{3},k_{4},s)z^{L(\sigma_{j},n)}}{L(\sigma_{j},n)},

(263)

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4};\Delta_{x}]}(z;k_{3},k_{4},s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{J}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)}(k_{3},k_{4},s)z^{L^{\prime}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)}}{L^{\prime}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)}.

(264)

The definition of
\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}(k_{3},k_{4},s)
and the value of
L
are given in (
226
), and

\mathcal{J}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)}
is defined analogously.
For the values of
L^{\prime}
, notice that the series expansion of the Bessel-
I
function in (
498
) is similar to that of the
Bessel-
K
function (
500
) but there are only
z^{\beta}
terms, so relative to Bessel-
K
we have
\sigma=1
.
Thus, the values of
L^{\prime}
are identical to
L
, but with
\sigma_{3}=1
fixed,

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)=L(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},+1,n)=\frac{\hat{d}}{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sigma_{j}\hat{\beta}_{j}+\hat{\beta}_{3}+2n,

(265)

and we define

L^{\prime}=L^{\prime}_{0}+\epsilon L^{\prime}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}},\quad L^{\prime}_{0}=\frac{d}{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sigma_{j}\beta_{j}+\beta_{3}+2n,\quad L^{\prime}_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}=u+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sigma_{j}v_{j}+v_{3}\,.

(266)

With this notation in place, we rewrite (
5.4.2
) as

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}=s^{-2\hat{\beta}_{x}}\hat{j}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{x}]}(k_{1},k_{2},s)\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{x}]}(k_{3},k_{4},s)

\displaystyle\qquad-s^{-2\hat{\beta}_{x}}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}=\pm 1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{I}_{(\sigma_{j},n)}(k_{3},k_{4},s)}{L(\sigma_{j},n)}\,\hat{j}_{-\hat{d}-1+L,[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{x}]}(k_{1},k_{2},s)

\displaystyle\qquad+s^{-2\hat{\beta}_{x}}\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}=\pm 1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathcal{J}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)}(k_{3},k_{4},s)}{L^{\prime}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},n)}\,\hat{i}_{-\hat{d}-1+L^{\prime},[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{x}]}(k_{1},k_{2},s).

(267)

Thus, the divergent terms
i^{\text{div}}
