(377)

Weight-shifting operators

In this section, we discuss the application of weight-shifting operators to relate correlators of different scaling dimensions.
In principle, such operators offer an elegant construction of the various correlators of interest starting from a smaller set of known ‘seed’ integrals.
In the cosmological context, the operators of
Karateev:2017jgd
were applied in
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Baumann:2019oyu
to construct 4-point correlators of conformally coupled and massless scalars in four-dimensional de Sitter. Holographically, these are dual to the three-dimensional CFT correlators of dimensions
\Delta=2
,
3
studied here.
However, there are several important subtleties associated to the use of weight-shifting operators that require careful consideration:

•

While weight-shifting operators can be applied to
connect amplitudes in the dimensionally regulated theory, their action
on exchange diagrams generates
a specific
linear combination
of shifted exchange and shifted contact diagrams as given in (
413
).
To arrive purely at a shifted exchange diagram, this shifted contact contribution must therefore be evaluated and subtracted off.
Sometimes, as we discuss, this shifted contact contribution can itself be constructed by acting with differential operators and symmetrizations.
Alternatively, when the shifted exchange diagram has a derivative vertex, the contact contribution is absent for certain special values of the operator dimensions. This occurs for the cases studied in
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Baumann:2019oyu
but is not a general phenomenon.

•

For other special values of the operator and spacetime dimensions, the coefficients parametrising the linear combination of shifted exchange and shifted contact diagrams can themselves vanish. In particular, this must happen whenever a shift operator connects a finite amplitude to a divergent amplitude. Clearly the result of acting with a differential operator on any finite amplitude must also be finite, and so any divergent shifted amplitude thus produced must be accompanied by a vanishing coefficient.
When this happens, knowledge of the finite amplitude only allows us to obtain the leading divergence of the shifted amplitude and not its subleading finite part.

•

After renormalization, the application of weight-shifting operators can no longer reliably connect different renormalized amplitudes. This is because the weight-shifting operators map only
homogeneous
solutions of the conformal Ward identities to shifted homogeneous solutions,
whereas the renormalized correlators obey
inhomogeneous
Ward identities containing anomalies and beta functions reflecting the breaking of conformal invariance.
As a specific example, it is impossible to obtain the amplitude
i_{[33,22x2]}
from
i_{[22,22x2]}
via the standard weight-shifting operators. This follows simply from the fact that
i_{[22,22x2]}
is anomaly-free whereas
i_{[33,22x2]}
is not, and the anomaly cannot be introduced through differential operators in the external momenta.
Thus, weight-shifting operators can only reliably be applied when working at the level of the regulated theory.

In this section we present a new momentum-space derivation of the relevant weight-shifting operator for scalar correlators. We then discuss their application to construct a reduction scheme providing an alternative route to the correlators we evaluated earlier by direct calculation of Witten diagrams. This shows how, with due care, the obstacles above can be avoided and the regulated amplitudes constructed, although the resulting scheme is not much simpler than evaluating the diagrams directly.

Shift operators from the shadow transform

The weight-shifting operators of principal interest are those shifting two of the conformal dimensions either up or down by one unit while preserving the spacetime dimension
d
.
Denoting these operators collectively as
\mathcal{W}_{ij}^{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}
, their action is to send

\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i}+\sigma_{i},\qquad\Delta_{j}\rightarrow\Delta_{j}+\sigma_{j},

(378)

where
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}\in\pm 1
represents any independent choice of signs and
(i,j)
are any pair of legs.
In momentum space, and acting on the first two legs, they take the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{12}^{2}

(379)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{+-}_{12}

\displaystyle=k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}k_{1}^{-2\beta_{1}}

(380)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{-+}_{12}

\displaystyle=k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}k_{2}^{-2\beta_{2}}

(381)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}

\displaystyle=k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}k_{1}^{-2\beta_{1}}k_{2}^{-2\beta_{2}}

(382)

where
\partial_{12}^{2}=\partial_{12}^{\mu}\partial_{12\mu}
with
\partial_{12\mu}=\partial/\partial k_{1}^{\mu}-\partial/\partial k_{2}^{\mu}
and
\beta_{i}=\Delta_{i}-d/2
as usual. The analogous expressions for other pairs of legs follow trivially through permutations.
These operators were introduced in position space in
Karateev:2017jgd
, and later applied in momentum space in
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Baumann:2019oyu
.
However, the compact expressions (
380
) - (
382
) are new and follow directly from the momentum-space shadow transform as we explain below. Upon multiplying out, one can verify that (
380
) - (
382
)
are equivalent to the expressions used in
Baumann:2019oyu
, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{-+}_{12}

\displaystyle=k_{2}^{2}\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}+2\beta_{2}\Big(\beta_{2}+1-\frac{d}{2}+k_{2}^{\mu}\partial_{12\mu}\Big)

(383)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{+-}_{12}

\displaystyle=k_{1}^{2}\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}+2\beta_{1}\Big(\beta_{1}+1-\frac{d}{2}-k_{1}^{\mu}\partial_{12\mu}\Big),

(384)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}

\displaystyle=k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}+2\beta_{1}\beta_{2}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-s^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+2\beta_{1}k_{2}^{2}\Big(\beta_{1}+1-\frac{d}{2}-k_{1}^{\mu}\partial_{12\mu}\Big)+2\beta_{2}k_{1}^{2}\Big(\beta_{2}+1-\frac{d}{2}+k_{2}^{\mu}\partial_{12\mu}\Big).

(385)

The weight-shifting action of these operators has been analyzed from an embedding space perspective in
Karateev:2017jgd
;
Baumann:2019oyu
. Here, we offer a simple alternative derivation based primarily in momentum space. Our analysis is restricted to the regulated theory to avoid divergences.

The action of the lowering operator
\mathcal{W}_{ij}^{--}
follows immediately since it is simply the Fourier transform (modulo a factor of one half) of
x_{ij}^{2}
. The latter acts as a lowering operator in position space since the
general position-space
n
-point function can be parametrised as

\langle\mathcal{O}(\bm{x}_{1})\ldots\mathcal{O}(\bm{x}_{n})\rangle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}x_{ij}^{2\alpha_{ij}}f(\bm{u}),

(386)

where the
f
is an arbitrary function of the independent cross ratios
\bm{u}
. In particular, inversions constrain the
\alpha_{ij}
to satisfy

\Delta_{i}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{ij},\qquad i=1,2,\ldots,n

(387)

where
\alpha_{ij}=\alpha_{ji}
and
\alpha_{ii}=0
.
Multiplying by
x_{ij}^{2}
thus shifts
\alpha_{ij}\rightarrow\alpha_{ij}+1
and hence we obtain a new solution with
\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i}-1
and
\Delta_{j}\rightarrow\Delta_{j}-1
but the same
f(\bm{u})
and spacetime dimension
d
.

Returning to momentum space, the action of this operator can also be seen from its intertwining relation with the conformal Ward identities.
By direct computation

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\mu}(\Delta_{1}-1)+\mathcal{K}_{2}^{\mu}(\Delta_{2}-1)\big)\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}=\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\mu}(\Delta_{1})+\mathcal{K}_{2}^{\mu}(\Delta_{2})\big),

(388)

where

\mathcal{K}^{\mu}_{i}(\Delta_{i})=k_{i}^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{i}^{\nu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{i\nu}}-2k_{i}^{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{i}^{\nu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{i\mu}}+2(\Delta_{i}-d)\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{i\mu}}

(389)

is the special conformal generator in momentum space. One can further check that
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
commutes with all remaining generators
\mathcal{K}^{\mu}_{i}(\Delta_{i})
for
i\neq 1,2
.
Given some solution
\mathcal{I}_{\{\Delta_{i}\}}
of the special conformal Ward identities,

0=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\mu}(\Delta_{i})\mathcal{I}_{\{\Delta_{i}\}},

(390)

by acting with
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
to the left it then follows that

0=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\mu}(\Delta_{i}-\delta_{i1}-\delta_{i2})\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}\mathcal{I}_{\{\Delta_{i}\}}.

(391)

Thus,
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}\mathcal{I}_{\{\Delta_{i}\}}
satisfies the shifted special conformal Ward identities obtained by replacing
\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i}-\delta_{i1}-\delta_{i2}
.
The corresponding shifted dilatation Ward identity is also satisfied as can be seen by noting that
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
is homogeneous with dimension
-2
.

The remaining operators (
381
) - (
382
) can then be constructed by applying the shadow transform. In momentum space, this takes the form of the operator identity

\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\mu}(d-\Delta_{i})k_{i}^{-2\beta_{i}}=k^{-2\beta_{i}}\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\mu}(\Delta_{i}).

(392)

Multiplying a given solution of the special conformal Ward identities by
k_{i}^{-2\beta_{i}}
thus generates a new solution with dimension
\Delta_{i}\rightarrow d-\Delta_{i}
. The corresponding dilatation Ward identity is also satisfied as can be verified by power counting.
To construct raising operators we then simply shadow transform a selection of momenta, apply the lowering operator
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
, and then invert the shadow transform.
For example,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}=k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}k_{1}^{-2\beta_{1}}k_{2}^{-2\beta_{2}},

(393)

since multiplying by
k_{1}^{-2\beta_{1}}k_{2}^{-2\beta_{2}}
sends
\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}_{i}=d-\Delta_{i}
for
i=1,2
, after which
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
sends
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{i}-1=d-1-\Delta_{i}
. The shadow transform is then inverted by applying
k_{1}^{-2\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}}k_{1}^{-2\beta_{2}^{\prime\prime}}=k_{1}^{-2\Delta_{1}^{\prime\prime}+d}k_{2}^{-2\Delta_{2}^{\prime\prime}+d}=k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}
sending
\Delta_{i}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\Delta_{i}^{\prime\prime\prime}=d-\Delta_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\Delta_{i}+1
. Thus, the net effect is to shift
\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i}+1
for
i=1,2
leaving all other dimensions intact. If instead we had shadow transformed with respect to only one of the momenta, say the first, then we would have obtained
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{+-}
since the action on
\Delta_{2}
would remain that of
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
.

As for
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
, we can prove the action of these operators more formally by verifying the intertwining relations.
For example, by direct calculation

\big(\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\mu}(\Delta_{1}+1)+\mathcal{K}_{2}^{\mu}(\Delta_{2}+1)\big)\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}=\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\mu}(\Delta_{1})+\mathcal{K}_{2}^{\mu}(\Delta_{2})\big)

(394)

while all other
\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\mu}(\Delta_{i})
with
i\neq 1,2
commute with
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
.
Thus, given some solution of the special conformal Ward identities (
390
), by acting to the left with
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
we find

0=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{K}_{i}^{\mu}(\Delta_{i}+\delta_{i1}+\delta_{i2})\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}\mathcal{I}_{\{\Delta_{i}\}},

(395)

and so
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
indeed generates a new solution with raised
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
. Again, the dilatation Ward identity is automatically satisfied since
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
is homogeneous of dimension
+2
. A similar analysis applies for
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{-+}
and
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{+-}
.

Action on exchange diagrams

To analyze the action of the shift operators (
379
) - (
382
) on the various amplitudes we study, it is convenient to convert to Mandelstam variables. In fact, it will suffice to consider their action on contact diagrams and
s
-channel exchanges only.
Since these amplitudes are all independent of
t
, we can then further simplify the shift operators by setting all
t
-derivatives to zero.
The operator
\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}
then takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}+\frac{(d-1)}{k_{1}}\partial_{1}+\frac{(d-1)}{k_{2}}\partial_{2}+\frac{1}{k_{1}k_{2}}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-s^{2})\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\Big]+O(\partial_{t}),

(396)

where
\partial_{i}=\partial/\partial k_{i}
while the remaining operators follow from (
380
) - (
382
).

Let us now apply
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}
to an
s
-channel exchange diagram (
•
‣
2
).
Acting on a product of bulk-boundary propagators (
3
), we find the identity

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{8(\beta_{1}-1)(\beta_{2}-1)}\Big(\Box_{z}-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-2)(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+d-2)\Big)\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}-1}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}-1}(z,k_{2})

(397)

where the box operator for
(d+1)
-dimensional AdS is

\Box_{z}=z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}+(1-d)z\partial_{z}-s^{2}z^{2}.

(398)

Thus, if we act with
\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}
on an
s
-channel exchange diagram, we obtain a box operator which can be integrated by parts
in
z
so as to act on the bulk-bulk propagator.
4
4

4

The boundary terms from
z\rightarrow\infty
vanish since all propagators decay exponentially.
The lower limit behaves as
\int\mathrm{d}z\,\partial_{z}z^{\omega+\epsilon}\sim\int\mathrm{d}z\,z^{\omega-1+\epsilon}

in the half-integer regularization scheme, where

\omega=d+\Delta_{x}-\tilde{\Delta}_{1}-\tilde{\Delta}_{2}
and
\tilde{\Delta}_{1}=\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1}
and
\tilde{\Delta}_{2}=\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2}
are the shifted dimensions.
For
\omega\neq-2m
with
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
this is zero by analytic continuation in
\epsilon
from the large-
\epsilon
region where it converges, see
Bzowski:2015pba
. Otherwise, we obtain a divergence which can be removed through a boundary counterterm.

This produces a delta function and a constant term, since the bulk-bulk propagator (
•
‣
2
) obeys

(-\Box_{z}+m_{x}^{2})\mathcal{G}_{d,\Delta_{x}}(z,s;\zeta)=z^{d+1}\delta(z-\zeta),\qquad m_{x}^{2}=\Delta_{x}(\Delta_{x}-d)=\beta_{x}^{2}-\frac{d^{2}}{4}.

(399)

In the regulated theory then, the action of
\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}
on an exchange diagram yields a linear combination of a shifted exchange and a shifted contact diagram,

\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}=\mathcal{N}^{--}_{exch.}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}-1,\Delta_{2}-1,\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}+\mathcal{N}^{--}_{cont.}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}-1,\Delta_{2}-1,\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]}

(400)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{--}_{exch.}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta_{x}^{2}-d^{2}/4-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-2)(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+d-2)}{8(\beta_{1}-1)(\beta_{2}-1)},

(401)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{--}_{cont.}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8(\beta_{1}-1)(\beta_{2}-1)}.

(402)

If our goal is to obtain just the shifted exchange diagram, we must therefore evaluate and subtract off this contribution from the shifted contact diagram. Sometimes this can be achieved through the further use of weight-shifting operators as discussed in Section
6.3.1
.

Let us next consider the action of the raising operator
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
on an
s
-channel exchange. This can easily be understood through use of (
397
) in combination with the shadow relation for the bulk-boundary propagator,

k^{-2\beta}\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta}(z,k)=\Lambda_{\beta}\,\mathcal{K}_{d,d-\Delta}(z,k),\qquad\Lambda_{\beta}=\frac{\Gamma(-\beta)}{4^{\beta}\Gamma(\beta)}.

(403)

First, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}k_{1}^{-2\beta_{1}}k_{2}^{-2\beta_{2}}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\beta_{1}}\Lambda_{\beta_{2}}k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,d-\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,d-\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\Lambda_{\beta_{1}}\Lambda_{\beta_{2}}k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}}{8(\beta_{1}+1)(\beta_{2}+1)}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(\Box_{z}-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+2)(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-d+2)\Big)\mathcal{K}_{d,d-\Delta_{1}-1}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,d-\Delta_{2}-1}(z,k_{2}).

(404)

We now commute the factor
k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}
through the differential operator and use again the shadow identity (
403
) (replacing
\Delta\rightarrow d-\Delta-1
) to find

\displaystyle k_{1}^{2(\beta_{1}+1)}k_{2}^{2(\beta_{2}+1)}\mathcal{K}_{d,d-\Delta_{1}-1}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,d-\Delta_{2}-1}(z,k_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad=\Lambda_{-\beta_{1}-1}\Lambda_{-\beta_{2}-1}\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}+1}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}+1}(z,k_{2}).

(405)

After simplifying the prefactor

\displaystyle\frac{\Lambda_{\beta_{1}}\Lambda_{\beta_{2}}}{8(\beta_{1}+1)(\beta_{2}+1)}\Lambda_{-\beta_{1}-1}\Lambda_{-\beta_{2}-1}=2\beta_{1}\beta_{2},

(406)

we arrive the desired identity

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=2\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\Big(\Box_{z}-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+2)(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-d+2)\Big)\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}+1}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}+1}(z,k_{2}).

(407)

This identity is the counterpart to (
397
) and can be used in the same way to evaluate the action of
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
on an
s
-channel exchange diagram. After integration by parts in
z
and use of (
399
),
we obtain the result

\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}=\mathcal{N}^{++}_{exch.}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2}+1,\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}+\mathcal{N}^{++}_{cont.}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2}+1,\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]}

(408)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{++}_{exch.}=2\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\Big(\beta_{x}^{2}-\frac{d^{2}}{4}-(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+2)(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}-d+2)\Big),\qquad\mathcal{N}^{++}_{cont.}

\displaystyle=-2\beta_{1}\beta_{2}.

(409)

The steps above can similarly be repeated for the remaining operators
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{+-}
and
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{-+}
.
Examining the form of these results, and those above, we find they can all be collected into a common form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)=\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Big(\Box_{z}-m^{2}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2}}(z,k_{2}).

(410)

where
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\pm 1
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}

\displaystyle=2^{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}-1}(\beta_{1})_{\sigma_{1}}(\beta_{2})_{\sigma_{2}},

(411)

\displaystyle m^{2}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{1}\beta_{1}+\sigma_{2}\beta_{2}+2)(\sigma_{1}\beta_{1}+\sigma_{2}\beta_{2}-d+2).

(412)

Here the Pochhammer symbol
(\beta)_{\sigma}=\Gamma(\beta+\sigma)/\Gamma(\beta)
is just a convenient way of writing
(\beta)_{1}=\beta
and
(\beta)_{-1}=(\beta-1)^{-1}
.
Acting on an
s
-channel exchange, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{12}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}=\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{exch.}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1},\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}+\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{cont.}\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1},\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]}

(413)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{exch.}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}(m_{x}^{2}-m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2}),\qquad\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{cont.}=-\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}.

(414)

Where divergences are present, this identity again applies only in the regulated theory.

Using the definition of
m_{x}^{2}
from (
399
), we can rewrite

\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{exch.}=-\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Big(\frac{d}{2}-\sigma_{1}\tilde{\beta}_{1}-\sigma_{2}\tilde{\beta}_{2}+\beta_{x}\Big)\Big(\frac{d}{2}-\sigma_{1}\tilde{\beta}_{1}-\sigma_{2}\tilde{\beta}_{2}-\beta_{x}\Big)

(415)

where the
\tilde{\beta}_{i}=\beta_{i}+\sigma_{i}
are the shifted values of the
\beta_{i}
parameters under
\Delta_{i}\rightarrow\Delta_{i}+\sigma_{i}
. From this expression, we see that
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{exch.}
has a zero whenever the condition

0=\frac{d}{2}-\sigma_{1}\tilde{\beta}_{1}-\sigma_{2}\tilde{\beta}_{2}-\sigma_{x}\beta_{x}

(416)

is satisfied for some choice of the signs
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{x})=(\pm,\pm,\pm)
, where each sign can be chosen independently of the others.
This makes sense since whenever this condition is satisfied the shifted exchange diagram is singular
5
5

5

The divergence of the shifted exchange diagram derives from the region where the vertex corresponding to the shifted operators approaches the boundary of AdS: effectively, this is the lower limit of an integral of three Bessel
K
functions, two of which come from the bulk-boundary propagators and the third from the exterior part of the bulk-bulk propagator. The singularities of such triple-
K
integrals, which also arise for the momentum-space 3-point function
\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{x}}\rangle\!\rangle
, have been analyzed in
Bzowski:2015pba
leading to precisely the condition (
416
).
, and a differential operator such as
\mathcal{W}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{12}
cannot map a finite exchange diagram to a singular one. Rather, acting with
\mathcal{W}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{12}
yields a finite result which, in the regulated theory, derives from the product of a vanishing coefficient
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{exch.}
and a divergent shifted exchange diagram, plus a contact contribution. In such situations where
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{exch.}
has a zero, the application of
\mathcal{W}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{12}
to a finite diagram therefore only allows us to extract the leading divergence of the shifted exchange diagram: to additionally compute its finite piece requires knowing the finite unshifted exchange diagram to subleading orders in the regulator
\epsilon
. This is generally harder to compute since the corresponding Bessel function indices are no longer half-integer.

For this reason, such divergences and their corresponding zeros in the exchange coefficients
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}_{exch.}
have important consequences for the construction of reduction schemes relating different exchange diagrams. Fortunately, as we will see in Section
6.4
, it turns out that for the correlators of interest in this paper, all such zeros of
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}_{exch.}
can be avoided through making appropriate use of shadow transformations in addition to the
\mathcal{W}^{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}_{ij}
operators.

Action on contact diagrams

As we saw above, the action of the
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
on an exchange diagram generates a linear combination of a shifted exchange diagram and a shifted contact diagram. The action of
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
on a contact diagram produces a linear combination of a shifted contact diagram and a shifted contract diagram containing an AdS box vertex.
In this section we discuss how this box contribution, which also follows from to the action of the Casimir on the shifted contact diagram, can in some cases be removed through an appropriate symmetrization. Further shift relations for contact diagrams are discussed in appendix
D.1
.
Once all shifted contact diagrams are known, either via weight-shifting operators or from direct evaluation, their contribution can be subtracted enabling all shifted exchange diagrams to be constructed.

Exchanges to contacts

As an intermediate step, one can always pass from an exchange diagram to the corresponding contact diagram (
i.e.,
with no shift to the operator dimensions) through the well-known action of the quadratic Casimir operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{12}

\displaystyle=\big(2k_{1\mu}k_{2\nu}-k_{1}\cdot k_{2}\,\delta_{\mu\nu}\big)\partial_{12}^{\mu}\partial_{12}^{\nu}+2\big((\Delta_{2}-d)k_{1\mu}-(\Delta_{1}-d)k_{2\mu}\big)\partial_{12}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\quad-(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-2d)(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}-d).

(417)

In Mandelstam variables,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{12}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}+k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})K_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-k_{1}^{2})K_{2}-\big(L_{1}+L_{2}+\frac{3d}{2}\big)^{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{4}+O(\partial_{t})

(418)

where

K_{i}=\partial_{i}^{2}+\frac{1-2\beta_{i}}{k_{i}}\partial_{i},\qquad L_{i}=k_{i}\partial_{i}-\Delta_{i}

(419)

and we have omitted
t
-derivatives since
s
-channel scalar exchanges are independent of
t
.
Indeed,
we can simplify this further since for
s
-channel scalar exchanges

(K_{1}-K_{2})\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}=0.

(420)

The action of the Casimir on such diagrams is thus equal to that of the reduced operator

\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{12}=\frac{s^{2}}{2}(K_{1}+K_{2})-\big(L_{1}+L_{2}+\frac{3d}{2}\big)^{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{4}.

(421)

We can also use this reduced Casimir operator when acting on scalar contact diagrams, since these are also independent of
t
(and
s
) and satisfy
(K_{1}-K_{2})\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]}=0
.

One can now verify directly the identity

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{12}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)=-\Box_{z}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big).

(422)

Acting on an
s
-channel exchange diagram with
(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{12}+m_{x}^{2})
and integrating by parts with respect to
z
, we obtain a delta function from (
399
). This collapses the exchange diagram to a contact diagram of the same operator dimensions:

\displaystyle\big(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{12}+m_{x}^{2}\big)\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}\,x\,\Delta_{x}]}=\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]}

(423)

One can alternatively obtain this result by showing that

\displaystyle[\mathcal{W}_{12}^{--},\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}]=2(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})\Big(\mathcal{C}_{12}+(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})^{2}-\frac{d}{2}(d-2)\Big),

(424)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{W}_{12}^{-+},\mathcal{W}_{12}^{+-}]=2(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})\Big(\mathcal{C}_{12}+(\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})^{2}-\frac{d}{2}(d-2)\Big),

(425)

then making two applications of (
413
).

Generating exchange diagrams with derivative vertices

Rather than integrating by parts the right-hand side of (
422
), we can also simply expand out the action of the AdS box (
398
) on the two bulk-boundary propagators. This produces a cross-term plus two terms where the box acts on a single bulk-boundary propagator.
The latter can be replaced via the equation of motion

(z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}+(1-d)z\partial_{z})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{i}}(z,k_{i})=(m_{i}^{2}+k_{i}^{2})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{i}}(z,k_{i}),\qquad m_{i}^{2}=\Delta_{i}(\Delta_{i}-d)

(426)

leading to the identity

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{12}+m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}\Big)\Big(\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)

(427)

\displaystyle\quad=z^{2}\Big(\partial_{z}\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\partial_{z}\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})+\frac{1}{2}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-s^{2})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\mathcal{K}_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big).

Since
(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-s^{2})/2=-\bm{k}_{1}\cdot\bm{k}_{2}
, the right-hand side here is precisely the vertex factor appearing in an exchange diagram with a
\Phi_{x}\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{1}\partial^{\mu}\Phi_{2}
derivative interaction, namely

\displaystyle i^{deriv}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{d+1}}z^{2}\Big(\partial_{z}\K_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\partial_{z}\K_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}-s^{2})\K_{d,\Delta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\K_{d,\Delta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{\zeta^{d+1}}\G_{d,\Delta_{x}}(z,s;\zeta)\K_{d,\Delta_{3}}(\zeta,k_{3})\K_{d,\Delta_{4}}(\zeta,k_{4}).

(428)

In the regulated theory, we can therefore generate an exchange diagram with a
\Phi_{x}\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{1}\partial^{\mu}\Phi_{2}
derivative vertex by acting on an ordinary exchange diagram with the Casimir operator:

\displaystyle\hat{i}^{deriv}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{12}+m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2})\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}.

(429)

Using (
423
), this exchange diagram with a derivative vertex can also be expressed as a sum of an ordinary exchange and a contact diagram,

\displaystyle\hat{i}^{deriv}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(m_{x}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}]}.

(430)

In certain special cases, this linear combination coincides with that produced by the action of the operator
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
in (
413
) leading to the relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{12}\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{=}}2\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\,\hat{i}^{deriv}_{[\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1},\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]},

(431)

where the coefficient
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
is that in (
411
).
The asterisk indicates that this equation is only valid when the masses satisfy the condition

m_{\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1}}^{2}+m_{\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2}}^{2}=m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2},

(432)

where
m_{\Delta_{i}+\sigma_{i}}^{2}=(\Delta_{i}+\sigma_{i})(\Delta_{i}+\sigma_{i}-d)
is the mass corresponding to the shifted dimension and

m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2}
is given in (
412
). This is equivalent to requiring

(\beta_{1}+\sigma_{1})^{2}+(\beta_{2}+\sigma_{2})^{2}-\frac{d^{2}}{2}=(\sigma_{1}\beta_{1}+\sigma_{2}\beta_{2}+2)(\sigma_{1}\beta_{1}+\sigma_{2}\beta_{2}+2-d).

(433)

In particular, this condition holds when
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
is applied to an exchange diagram of conformal scalars with
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}=2
in
d=3
. In this case
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=1/2
,
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=1
and all the masses in (
432
) vanish individually. The action of
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{++}
then leads to an exchange diagram where two massless scalars are derivatively coupled to a conformal scalar
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Baumann:2019oyu
.

Shifted contact diagrams from symmetrization

Let us now return to consider the action of
\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}
on the contact diagram
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]}
. From (
410
), we obtain a linear combination of the shifted contact diagram and a shifted contact diagram with an AdS box vertex. Via (
422
), the latter can be re-expressed as the Casimir operator acting on the shifted contact diagram giving

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Big(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{12}^{\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1},\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2}}+m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2}\Big)\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}+\sigma_{1},\Delta_{2}+\sigma_{2},\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}]},

(434)

where
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}
and
m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2}
are given in (
411
) and (
412
) but the dimensions appearing inside the Casimir operator are the shifted ones as indicated by the superscript indices.

In the special case where at least three of the shifted operator dimensions are equal, the Casimir operator on the right-hand side of this relation can now be eliminated through a simple symmetrization procedure.
Here, it is useful to recall that from an embedding space perspective, the
d
-dimensional conformal Ward identities originate from invariance under the action of the
SO(d+1,1)
Lorentz generators

0=\sum_{i=1}^{4}L^{AB}_{i}

(435)

where
L_{i}^{AB}=X_{i}^{A}(\partial/\partial X_{iB})-X_{i}^{B}(\partial/\partial X_{iA})
.
Squaring this relation and using the identities

\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}}=\frac{1}{2}(L_{i}^{AB}+L_{j}^{AB})(L_{iAB}+L_{jAB}),\qquad\frac{1}{2}L_{i}^{AB}L_{iAB}=-\Delta_{i}(\Delta_{i}-d),

(436)

along with

\mathcal{C}_{12}^{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}=\mathcal{C}_{34}^{\Delta_{3},\Delta_{4}},\qquad\mathcal{C}_{14}^{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{4}}=\mathcal{C}_{23}^{\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}},\qquad\mathcal{C}_{13}^{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{3}}=\mathcal{C}_{24}^{\Delta_{2},\Delta_{4}},

(437)

as follows from squaring,
e.g.,

L_{1}^{AB}+L_{2}^{AB}=-(L_{3}^{AB}+L_{4}^{AB})
,
we obtain

\mathcal{C}_{12}^{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}}+\mathcal{C}_{13}^{\Delta_{1},\Delta_{3}}+\mathcal{C}_{23}^{\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i<j}^{4}\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{\Delta_{i},\Delta_{j}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Delta_{i}(\Delta_{i}-d).

(438)

If the three operator dimensions on the left-hand side are equal, this reduces to

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{C}_{12}^{\Delta,\Delta}+\mathcal{C}_{13}^{\Delta,\Delta}+\mathcal{C}_{23}^{\Delta,\Delta}\Big)\hat{i}_{[\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}=-\Big(3\Delta(\Delta-d)+\Delta_{4}(\Delta_{4}-d)\Big)\,\hat{i}_{[\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}.

(439)

Moreover, we know from (
434
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\hat{i}_{[\Delta-\sigma_{1},\Delta-\sigma_{2},\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Big(\mathcal{C}_{12}^{\Delta,\Delta}+m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2}\Big)\hat{i}_{[\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}.

(440)

By permuting momenta and exploiting the fact that the three shifted dimensions are equal, we can now construct all the remaining terms on the left-hand side of (
439
). For example,

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\hat{i}_{[\Delta-\sigma_{1},\Delta-\sigma_{2},\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}\Big)_{\bm{k}_{2}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Big(\mathcal{C}_{13}^{\Delta,\Delta}+m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2}\Big)\hat{i}_{[\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}.

(441)

Summing over permutations, the Casimir contributions can now be eliminated

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{W}_{12}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\hat{i}_{[\Delta-\sigma_{1},\Delta-\sigma_{2},\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}\Big)+(\bm{k}_{2}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{3})+(\bm{k}_{1}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\Big(3\Delta(\Delta-d)+\Delta_{4}(\Delta_{4}-d)-3m_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}^{2}\Big)\hat{i}_{[\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}

\displaystyle\qquad=2^{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}-1}(\beta-\sigma_{1})_{\sigma_{1}}(\beta-\sigma_{2})_{\sigma_{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Big(3\beta^{2}+\beta_{4}^{2}-d^{2}-3\beta^{2}(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})^{2}+3\beta d(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})\Big)\hat{i}_{[\Delta,\Delta,\Delta,\Delta_{4}]}

(442)

where the Pochammer symbols are simply
(\beta-1)_{1}=\beta-1
and
(\beta+1)_{-1}=\beta^{-1}
.
This relation (and its permutations) thus enable the construction of shifted contact diagrams containing three equal dimensions if the appropriate initial integral is known. Some further shift relations for contact diagrams can be found in appendix
D.1
.

Reduction scheme

Equation (
413
) can be used to set up a reduction scheme. Starting from a smaller set of simple master integrals, one can use the weight-changing operators to obtain other diagrams. Since the lower dimensions generally yield simpler expressions, one would like to start with the lowest dimensional diagrams such as
i_{[22,22x\Delta_{x}]}
and apply the weight-raising operators
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{ij}
. To see if it is possible, we begin by investigating all available relations due to (
413
) between the amplitudes.

Available relations

First, notice that one can either use the weight-changing operators directly or in a combination with the shadow transform. The shadow transform is an observation that the bulk-to-boundary propagators for the AdS fields with
\Delta
and
d-\Delta
are related according to,

\displaystyle\K_{d,d-\Delta}(z,k)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\Delta-\tfrac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\tfrac{d}{2}-\Delta\right)}\left(\frac{k}{2}\right)^{d-2\Delta}\times\K_{d,\Delta}(z,k).

(443)

Thus, we can define the shadow transform
\mathcal{S}_{j}
acting on the
j
-th external leg of an amplitude as the multiplication by the above constant. This yields, for example,

\displaystyle i_{[d-\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{1}i_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\Delta_{1}-\tfrac{d}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\tfrac{d}{2}-\Delta_{1}\right)}\left(\frac{k_{1}}{2}\right)^{d-2\Delta_{1}}\times i_{[\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}.

(444)

It is important to use regulated dimensions
\hat{d}
and
\hat{\Delta}_{1}
when dealing with regulated amplitudes.

While the weight-shifting operators
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{ij}
and
\mathcal{W}^{--}_{ij}
always raise or lower a pair of dimensions, we can compose them with the shadow transform to raise or lower a single dimension only. As an example, consider
i_{[22,22x2]}
. By acting with
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}
, according to (
413
) one should obtain a combination of
i_{[33,22x2]}
and
i_{[3322]}
. On the other hand, we can use the shadow transform to flip
\Delta_{2}=2
to
d-\Delta_{2}=1
. Thus, the combination
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}\circ\mathcal{S}_{2}
should map
i_{[22,22x2]}
to a combination of
i_{[32,22x2]}
and
i_{[3222]}
. In general, we can carry out the following operations on a pair of dimensions:

(22)
(33)
(32)
\mathcal{W}^{++}
\mathcal{W}^{--}
\mathcal{W}^{++}\mathcal{S}_{2}
\mathcal{S}_{2}\mathcal{W}^{--}

(445)

It is always important to remember that the action of the weight-shifting operators on an exchange diagram results in a combination of shifted exchange and contact diagrams.

The scheme

At first glance, all exchange amplitudes with a fixed exchange dimension
\Delta_{x}
are related through repeated use of the operations in (
445
). On practical level, however, this turns out not to be the case. In order for the reduction scheme to work, three conditions must be satisfied:

1.

One must know all contact diagrams featuring on the right hand side of (
413
). These are easy to calculate directly and thus pose no difficulty.

Alternatively, one can attempt to construct them via the shift relation (
6.3.3
) starting from
\hat{i}_{[2323]}
. This contact diagram can itself be obtained from either
\hat{i}_{[2323x2]}
or
\hat{i}_{[2323x3]}
via (
423
).
First, we evaluate

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{24}^{--}\hat{i}_{[2323]})+(\bm{k}_{2}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{3})+(\bm{k}_{3}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{4})

\displaystyle=-(2+\epsilon)(5+2\epsilon)\hat{i}_{[2222]},

(446)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{13}^{++}\hat{i}_{[2323]})+(\bm{k}_{2}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{3})+(\bm{k}_{3}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon(3-2\epsilon)\hat{i}_{[3333]}.

(447)

In this last equation,
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{13}
projects out the leading
\epsilon^{-1}
divergence of
\hat{i}_{[2323]}
meaning the left-hand side is of order
\epsilon^{0}
. We can therefore only construct the leading
\epsilon^{-1}
divergence of
\hat{i}_{[3333]}
from this relation.
Next, via the shadow transform (
443
), we find

\hat{i}_{[1323]}=\mathcal{S}_{1}\hat{i}_{[2323]}=\frac{4^{\beta_{1}}\Gamma(\beta_{1})}{\Gamma(-\beta_{1})}k_{1}^{-2\beta_{1}}\hat{i}_{[2323]}=-\frac{1}{k_{1}}\hat{i}_{[2323]}.

(448)

We now construct

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{12}^{+-}\hat{i}_{[1323]})+(\bm{k}_{2}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{3})+(\bm{k}_{1}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{3})

\displaystyle=(3+6\epsilon+2\epsilon^{2})\hat{i}_{[2223]},

(449)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{W}_{23}^{++}\hat{i}_{[2223]})+(\bm{k}_{2}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{4})+(\bm{k}_{3}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}_{4})

\displaystyle=(-1+3\epsilon-2\epsilon^{2})\hat{i}_{[2333]}.

(450)

From
\hat{i}_{[2323]}
, we can thus obtain all the contact diagrams of weight two and three operators to finite order, with the exception of
\hat{i}_{[3333]}
which we only know to leading
\epsilon^{-1}
order.
The finite part of this integral can be found either by direction evaluation or through other shift operators as discussed in appendix
D.1
.

2.

Regularization must be taken care of. For example, consider
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}i_{[22,22x2]}
, which we denote by
i_{[\tilde{3}\tilde{3},22x2]}
. Since
i_{[22,22x2]}
is finite, so is
i_{[\tilde{3}\tilde{3},22x2]}
. However, we see that both the exchange diagram
i_{[33,22x2]}
in (
34
) as well as the contact diagram
i_{[3322]}
in (
26
) are divergent. Nevertheless, their combination on the right-hand side of (
413
) is finite in any regularization scheme and

\displaystyle\left[-1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}(3u-7v_{1}-7v_{2}+v_{x})+O(\epsilon^{2})\right]\hat{i}_{[33,22x2]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left[-\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon(v_{1}+v_{2})+O(\epsilon^{2})\right]\hat{i}_{[3322]}=i_{[\tilde{3}\tilde{3},22x2]}.

(451)

Thus, one can derive
\hat{i}_{[33,22x2]}
regulated in an arbitrary scheme once the regulated contact diagram
\hat{i}_{[3322]}
is known in that scheme. It is important to emphasize that, whenever regularization is required, (
413
) holds for regulated amplitudes. If the terms of order
\epsilon
in the above expression were dropped, the equality would fail.

Indeed, if we naively apply weight-shifting operators to renormalized diagrams, due to the presence of counterterm contributions the two sides of (
413
) will generally only agree up to some local terms.
For example,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}i^{\text{ren}}_{[22,22x2]}

\displaystyle=-i^{\text{ren}}_{[33,22x2]}-\frac{1}{2}i^{\text{ren}}_{[3322]}+\frac{k_{3}+k_{4}}{8}\left(3+2\mathfrak{a}_{[3322]}^{(1)}-2\mathfrak{a}_{[33,22x2]}^{(1)}\right),

(452)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}i^{\text{ren}}_{[22,33x2]}

\displaystyle=-i^{\text{ren}}_{[33,33x2]}-\frac{1}{2}i^{\text{ren}}_{[3333]}+\frac{k_{1}^{3}+k_{2}^{3}}{24}\left(3-2\mathfrak{a}_{[3333]}^{(1)}+2\mathfrak{a}_{[33,33x2]}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{k_{3}^{3}+k_{4}^{3}}{24}\left(-3-2\mathfrak{a}_{[3333]}^{(1)}+2\mathfrak{a}_{[33,33x2]}^{(1)}\right).

(453)

The moral is that
(
413
) holds for regulated diagrams and one should be wary of
extrapolating this relation to renormalized diagrams.

3.

The value of the constant
\mathcal{N}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{exch.}
on the right hand side of (
413
) cannot vanish when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. The constant vanishes when (
416
) is satisfied for unregulated parameters. In such a case the finite part of
\mathcal{W}^{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}_{12}\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
does not allow for the determination of the finite part of the exchange diagram
\hat{i}_{[\Delta_{1}+1,\Delta_{2}+1,\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}x\Delta_{x}]}
. In cases of
\Delta_{j}=2,3
,
j=1,2,3,4,x
considered in this paper and
d=3
this precludes the use of half of the operations listed in (
445
). The operations that still can be used in the scheme, depending on the value of the exchange dimension
\Delta_{x}
are presented in the following table:

\mathcal{W}_{ij}

\mathcal{W}^{--}

\mathcal{W}^{++}\mathcal{S}

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{W}^{--}

\Delta_{x}=2

yes

no

no

yes

\Delta_{x}=3

no

yes

yes

no

This means, for example, that the exchange diagram
\hat{i}_{[33,22x3]}
cannot be obtained from
\hat{i}_{[22,22x3]}
by the application of (
413
). Indeed, in such a case
\mathcal{N}^{++}_{exch.}=\tfrac{3\epsilon}{2}(u-v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{x})+O(\epsilon^{2})
and vanishes in the
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
limit.

Taking into account the above considerations, a workable reduction scheme connecting the various amplitudes is presented in Figures
5
and
6
.
Given the seed integral
\hat{i}_{[32,32x2]}
we can construct all the integrals with
\Delta_{x}=2
, and from
\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}
we obtain all the integrals with
\Delta_{x}=3
.

i_{[22,22x2]}
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{34}
\hat{i}_{[22,33x2]}
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}
\hat{i}_{[33,33x2]}
\hat{i}_{[32,22x2]}
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{34}
\hat{i}_{[32,33x2]}
\mathcal{S}_{2}\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}
\mathcal{S}_{2}\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}
\hat{i}_{[32,32x2]}
\mathcal{S}_{4}\mathcal{W}^{--}_{34}

Figure 5:
Reduction scheme for the amplitudes with
\Delta_{x}=2
.

i_{[22,22x3]}
\mathcal{W}^{--}_{34}
\hat{i}_{[22,33x3]}
\mathcal{W}^{--}_{12}
\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}
\hat{i}_{[32,22x3]}
\mathcal{W}^{--}_{34}
\hat{i}_{[32,33x3]}
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}\mathcal{S}_{2}
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{12}\mathcal{S}_{2}
\hat{i}_{[32,32x3]}
\mathcal{W}^{++}_{34}\mathcal{S}_{4}

Figure 6:
Reduction scheme for the amplitudes with
\Delta_{x}=3
.

Shifting the dimension of the exchanged operator

To complete our analysis, we now seek a weight-shifting operator capable of connecting the two schemes in Figures
5
and
6
, so that only a single exchange diagram is required as a seed integral. This requires shifting the dimension
\Delta_{x}
of the exchanged operator.

For exchange diagrams with all external dimensions equal to two, a shift operator sending
\Delta_{x}\rightarrow\Delta_{x}+1
was given in
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
. A detailed derivation of this operator (
560
) is given in appendix
D.2
. Here, however, this operator is not suitable since the direction of the arrows in Figure
6
is such that connecting
\hat{i}_{[22,22x2]}
to
\hat{i}_{[22,22x3]}
does not allow us to recover the three ‘upstream’ integrals
\hat{i}_{[22,33x3]}
,
\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}
and
\hat{i}_{[32,33x3]}
. We will therefore follow a different route, deriving a new shift operator based on the properties of Bessel functions that will allow us to recover
\hat{i}_{[32,32x2]}
, and hence all the
\Delta_{x}=2
integrals, starting from the
\Delta_{x}=3
integrals.

In the analysis to follow, it will be clearer to write the AdS propagators as functions of the spacetime dimension
d
and the parameter
\beta_{i}=\Delta_{i}-d/2
. To this end, we re-define

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\beta_{i}}(z,k_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{z^{d/2}k_{i}^{\beta}K_{\beta_{i}}(k_{i}z)}{2^{\beta_{i}-1}\Gamma(\beta_{i})},

(454)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{d,\beta_{x}}(z,s;\zeta)

\displaystyle=(z\zeta)^{d/2}\Big(K_{\beta_{x}}(sz)I_{\beta_{x}}(s\zeta)\Theta(z-\zeta)+I_{\beta_{x}}(sz)K_{\beta_{x}}(s\zeta)\Theta(\zeta-z)\Big)

(455)

where the tildes remind us of the change of argument from
(d,\Delta_{i})
to
(d,\beta_{i})
.
From the elementary properties of Bessel functions, the bulk-boundary propagator obeys the relation

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d-2,\,\frac{1}{2}}(z,k_{i})=\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\,\frac{3}{2}}(z,k_{i})-k_{i}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\,\frac{1}{2}}(z,k_{i}).

(456)

Writing the exchange diagram as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(d)}_{[\beta_{1},\beta_{2};\beta_{3},\beta_{4};x\beta_{x}]}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z^{d+1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\zeta}{\zeta^{d+1}}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\beta_{1}}(z,k_{1})\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\beta_{2}}(z,k_{2})\tilde{G}_{d,\beta_{x}}(z,s;\zeta)\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\beta_{3}}(\zeta,k_{3})\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\beta_{4}}(\zeta,k_{4}),

(457)

and using this relation twice, we then find that

\mathcal{I}^{(d-2)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\,x\frac{3}{2}]}=\mathcal{I}^{(d)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2};\,x\frac{3}{2}]}-k_{2}\mathcal{I}^{(d)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2};\,x\frac{3}{2}]}-k_{4}\mathcal{I}^{(d)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\,x\frac{3}{2}]}+k_{2}k_{4}\mathcal{I}^{(d)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\,x\frac{3}{2}]}.

(458)

Here, we exploited the fact that the factor of
(z\zeta)^{-d}
from the two integration measures cancels with the factors of
z^{d/2}
and
\zeta^{d/2}
from the first and third bulk-boundary propagators and the factor of
(z\zeta)^{d/2}
from the bulk-bulk propagator. A shift
d\rightarrow d-2
on the second and fourth bulk-boundary propagators thus extends to the entire exchange diagram.

Meanwhile, the bulk-bulk propagator obeys the identity

\frac{1}{2}\Big(z^{2}+\zeta^{2}-\partial_{s}^{2}+(2\sigma_{x}\beta_{x}-1)\frac{1}{s}\partial_{s}\Big)\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{d-2,\beta_{x}}(z,s;\zeta)=\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{d,\beta_{x}+\sigma_{x}}(z,s;\zeta),\quad\sigma_{x}=\pm 1,

(459)

which shifts the spacetime dimension up by two while increasing or decreasing
\beta_{x}
by one.
To apply this operator to
s
-channel exchange diagrams, we further replace the factors of
z^{2}
and
\zeta^{2}
with Bessel operators acting on the external legs,

\Big(\partial_{i}^{2}+\frac{1-2\beta_{i}}{k_{i}}\partial_{i}\Big)\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\beta_{i}}(z,k_{i})=z^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{d,\beta_{i}}(z,k_{i}).

(460)

So long as we choose one external leg corresponding to each vertex the result is the same. Choosing legs 2 and 4, this gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(d)}_{[\beta_{1},\beta_{2};\beta_{3},\beta_{4};x\beta_{x}+\sigma_{x}]}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\partial_{2}^{2}+\frac{1-2\beta_{2}}{k_{2}}\partial_{2}+\partial_{4}^{2}+\frac{1-2\beta_{4}}{k_{4}}\partial_{4}-\partial_{s}^{2}+(2\sigma_{x}\beta_{x}-1)\frac{1}{s}\partial_{s}\Big)\mathcal{I}^{(d-2)}_{[\beta_{1},\beta_{2};\beta_{3},\beta_{4};x\beta_{x}]}

(461)

Thus, applying this operator with
\sigma_{x}=-1
to the left-hand side of (
458
), we find

\mathcal{I}^{(d)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\,x\frac{1}{2}]}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\partial_{2}^{2}+\partial_{4}^{2}-\partial_{s}^{2}-\frac{4}{s}\partial_{s}\Big)\mathcal{I}^{(d-2)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\,x\frac{3}{2}]}.

(462)

Using (
458
) and (
462
), we can now obtain the
\Delta_{x}=2
master integral
\hat{i}_{[32,32x2]}
starting from the
\Delta_{x}=3
master integral
\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}
.
In the first step, we compute all the
\Delta_{x}=3
integrals using the reduction scheme illustrated in Figure
6
. The right-hand side of (
458
) can then be constructed in the half-integer scheme:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(1+2\epsilon)}_{[\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2};\,x\frac{3}{2}]}

\displaystyle=\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}-k_{2}\hat{i}_{[32,33x3]}-k_{4}\hat{i}_{[33,32x3]}+k_{2}k_{4}\hat{i}_{[32,32x3]}.

(463)

Equation (
462
) now gives the desired result

\displaystyle\hat{i}_{[32,32x2]}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\partial_{2}^{2}+\partial_{4}^{2}-\partial_{s}^{2}-\frac{4}{s}\partial_{s}\Big)\Big(\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}-k_{2}\hat{i}_{[32,33x3]}-k_{4}\hat{i}_{[33,32x3]}+k_{2}k_{4}\hat{i}_{[32,32x3]}\Big).

(464)

Using this relation in combination with the reduction schemes in Figures
5
and
6
we can thus obtain all exchange diagrams starting from only the exchange diagram
\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}
and contact diagrams. As the most complicated integral,
\hat{i}_{[33,33x3]}
is the natural starting point since the action of differential operators results in a reduction in complexity (
i.e.,
a lowering of the degree of transcendality and/or a reduction in the order of polynomial coefficients).

, whose contents are described

below.

7.2
.

The package

The package
HandbooK.wl
provides explicit expressions for both regulated and renormalized 2-, 3-, and 4-point functions.

command:

For example, the amplitude
\hat{i}_{[222]}
regulated in the half-integer scheme with
u=1
and
v_{j}=0
can be obtained by the command

By default, the regulator is represented by
\epsilon
while the momentum magnitudes are
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}
and
s
for the Mandelstam variable.

If no regulator is specified, the half-integer scheme is assumed by default. Thus, for example,
iReg3pt[3, {2,2,2}]
is equivalent to
iReg3pt[
3+2\epsilon,\{2+\epsilon,2+\epsilon,2+\epsilon\}
]
evaluated above and returns the amplitude in the half-integer scheme.

•

By default the amplitude is expanded to the highest available order in the regulator. Since the 3-point amplitudes regulated in the half-integer scheme are exact, the expression above is not expanded in
\epsilon
at all. In a general regularization scheme, the expressions are expanded to order
\epsilon^{1}

The explicit expressions for 4-point exchange amplitudes can be quite involved, particularly in a general regularization scheme. This is due to the use of the equation (
270
) and the complicated structure of the scheme changing terms
i^{\text{div}}(u,v_{j})-i^{\text{div}}(1,0)
. As an example, consider the amplitude
\hat{i}_{[33,22x2]}
regulated in the scheme where
\hat{d}=3-\epsilon
while conformal dimension
\Delta_{j}
are not regulated for all
j=1,2,3,4,x
. The amplitude can be accessed by the command

one can prevent the scheme-changing terms from expanding. This produces results similar in presentation to those given in Section
5.5
, as illustrated in Figure
7
.

command.
